Abstract: Small open-weight language models can run crowd decision-making (CDM) on an institution's own infrastructure, which keeps the checkpoint, the inference settings and the opinions under its control. Their judgements vary across repeated queries; whether repeating and aggregating them, often through the 2-tuple linguistic representation, settles the decision rather than the labels is untested. Five open-weight models of 8-36B total parameters on one consumer GPU and a hosted reference classify 474 TripAdvisor reviews of four London restaurants ten times each; three open-weight models repeat the protocol on 600 reviews of twelve Madrid restaurants. Continuous evaluations preserve resolution: snapping each review leaves 5 distinct values against 21-65, and snapping at every level at most two collective values. Sensitivity to the participants persists once run-to-run variability is reduced: in London, redrawing the reviewers keeps the winner on 47-81% of replicas against 86-100% for redrawing the runs. With simultaneous intervals over every pair of alternatives under crowd resampling, no system on either corpus names a single winner: in London every system keeps all four restaurants as candidates, and in Madrid the candidate sets hold five to eight of the twelve. Extra runs and output-level fusion stabilize the winner but do not remove that sensitivity, and the fusion does not beat its best member. The three larger open-weight models name the hosted reference's London winner and at least match its per-review correlation with the reviewers' stars, so small open-weight models can support local CDM, reported as candidate sets with margins rather than forced rankings.

</details>

Abstract: Self-supervised speech models (S3Ms) have been scaled up to thousands of languages and millions of hours of audio, yet little is known about how these models structure semantic knowledge across languages. In this work, we probe sentence-level semantics in massively multilingual S3Ms across 76 languages, using linear maps from frozen speech representations into a frozen text embedding space, evaluated by cross-modal retrieval. Sentence meaning is linearly accessible but highly uneven across languages and resource tiers. Cross-lingual probes can match language-specific probes on languages never seen in probe training, but per-model monolingual decodability does not predict transfer. Within the OmniASR wav2vec family, scaling from 300M--7B disproportionately benefits high-resource languages, and ASR tuning sharply improves semantic decodability while making representations more language-specific. However, semantic content is entangled with acoustics, inflating apparent performance on single-speaker corpora.

</details>

Abstract: Policy gradient methods are widely used to align large language model outputs with human preferences, but the log-likelihoods they require cannot be directly computed in Masked Diffusion Language Models (MDLMs). MDLM post-training therefore relies on a surrogate objective based on the Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO), and recent work estimates a blockwise variant of this objective via Monte Carlo methods to support blockwise generation. However, existing estimation schemes randomly sample the block index, so they may evaluate the same block more than once and leave some blocks unevaluated. This introduces variance arising from block selection into the estimates. In this work, we propose a full-block estimator that evaluates every block exactly once and averages the results. We theoretically show that the proposed estimator remains unbiased while eliminating the between-group variance arising from block selection, thereby achieving lower variance than the baseline for the same sample size. In experiments on five benchmarks spanning mathematics, planning, and coding, our method achieves the highest accuracy on four benchmarks and outperforms the random block sampling baseline by an average of 12.79 points.

</details>

Completeness and exclusivity criteria require set-valued answers to be found in full and with nothing extra. We rank models by strict pass@1, the share of attempts that meet every criterion. Across 20 model configurations, including frontier models from Google, Anthropic, and OpenAI, the top-scoring one, Gemini 4 Argon, passes only 38.3% of attempts, and the median score is 19.3%, leaving wide headroom. Abstract: Typhoon is an audiovisual installation based on Joseph Conrad's novella, in which Captain MacWhirr steers the steamer Nan-Shan directly into and through a catastrophic storm. The novella is a study of what happens when the scale of a phenomenon exceeds the practical and conceptual frameworks available to comprehend it and of the paralysis that sometimes follows a demand for action in such overwhelming circumstances. We chose the text of Typhoon to reflect on human culture's current AI moment. We prompt a large language model, seeded with Conrad's original language, to feed endlessly on its own output, and explore when and how the text becomes increasingly alien and unwieldy as time progresses, including deviations from the narrative, digressions into the model's own instructions and thinking, and long periods of incoherent rumination. We employ methods from activation steering, including a novel adaptive steering method we introduce, to control the rhythm of the narrative and the model's digressions over long stretches of generation. The simultaneous efficacy and unpredictability of this steering showcases the tension between the perceived interpretability of AI, and the reality of a system that remains outside our comprehension and control.

</details>

Abstract: Frontier LLM agents that query databases with tools are among the most accurate text-to-SQL systems, but per-query calls to a hosted model are slow, costly, and expose sensitive organizational queries to external APIs. For example, a Claude Opus 5.5 agent serving 10,000 queries a day costs over $70,000 a year at list price. To what extent can local hardware already owned by an organization offload this burden without compromising the agent's accuracy? We present LuthorSQL, a non-autoregressive 7B text-to-SQL model that serves as a rapid System 1 gate for a slower LLM agent (System 2), escalating queries only when uncertain. LuthorSQL makes a single forward pass to predict slot scores over a bounded grammar for query plans, compiles and runs up to 32 candidate plans, and chooses an answer using a trained validator. On its own, LuthorSQL reaches 68.03% execution accuracy on BIRD's hidden test set at 0.84 s per question on a single GPU, maintains its accuracy in 9 GB with 8-bit quantized weights, and transfers zero-shot to Snowflake, BigQuery, MySQL, PostgreSQL, and DuckDB within 3.1 points of its native SQLite accuracy. Combined with Claude Opus 5.5 in a pre-registered confidence cascade over 227 questions on databases published after Opus's training cutoff, LuthorSQL answers nearly half of all questions locally and cuts API cost by 33.3% at the same accuracy. Finally, contamination probing shows that Opus reproduces 48-92% of public benchmark gold queries verbatim without seeing the schema; however, its tool-assisted accuracy holds on renamed schemas and reaches 95.6-97.4% on post-cutoff databases. Code and data: https://github.com/IAMIbrahimmemon/luthorsql

</details>

Abstract: In retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), query rewriting serves as a core interface that aligns user intent with external knowledge bases, and its effectiveness directly determines overall system performance. However, generative retrieval faces three critical challenges in real-world deployment: noisy retrieval feedback, the difficulty of jointly optimizing discrete prompt templates and continuous decoding-control parameters, and the limited generalization of static rewriting strategies under semantically complex scenarios. To address these issues, we propose RAG-FGO, a dynamic optimization framework for generative retrieval strategies. RAG-FGO formulates query rewriting as a non-differentiable black-box optimization problem in a mixed discrete-continuous space, and represents prompt templates and decoding parameters as adaptively optimizable query-rewriting agents. The framework performs global search to enable semantic jumps and structural reorganization that escape local optima, while applying local perturbations for fine-grained alignment and precise refinement. In addition, RAG-FGO introduces an elite-preserving memory pool that accumulates high-quality strategies across iterations, thereby mitigating the impact of stochasticity and noisy feedback on optimization stability. Experimental results on a diverse suite of benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed method, supporting its applicability to complex semantic retrieval tasks.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used by reviewers to highlight weaknesses during the reviewing process, and their outputs often shape the reviews that humans submit. Prior work studies how LLM reviews align with human ones, yet we know little about which parts of a paper these models actually criticize. In this work, we investigate 2,000 papers from ACL, EMNLP, COLM, and ICLR, and show that the latest GPT and Claude models draw about 50% or more of their review weaknesses from the appendix. This behavior holds across the API, coding-agent, and web access methods for using LLMs to review a paper. In contrast, we find that humans draw only 8.4% of their weaknesses from the appendix, and these weaknesses primarily revolve around a paper's presentation and content related to its core idea. Meanwhile, LLMs draw weaknesses from deep in the appendix that concern fine-grained technical details not central to the paper's contributions. We anticipate that our analysis will contribute to the scholarly discourse on AI reviews. Our code is publicly available at: https://github.com/cincynlp/review_appendix.

</details>

Abstract: Long-horizon LLM agents can coordinate tools and applications to complete complex, multi-step tasks. As their interaction histories grow, effective context management becomes essential for reliable and efficient execution. Existing methods reduce context through summarization, pruning, or history selection. However, shorter contexts do not necessarily support better decisions: summaries can omit exact details needed for later actions, while retaining entire interactions can carry substantial irrelevant information. We propose CarryOn, a lightweight context manager that pairs compact execution state with fine-grained, source-linked evidence. A learned selector and adaptive retention policy keep relevant evidence available without additional LLM calls for context management. We introduce two complementary evaluation metrics: $S_α$ prioritizes task success while accounting for resource use, and normalized cost per success (NCS) measures the resources spent per successful task. Across AppWorld, OfficeBench, and Tau2 Telecom with three LLM Actors, CarryOn achieves 75.7% macro-averaged task success, exceeding the strongest baseline by 6.7 percentage points. It also achieves the best average scores on both metrics.

</details>

Abstract: As AI conference submissions grow rapidly, AI reviewers are being considered as an alternative. However, simply imitating human reviewing is problematic because reviewing serves multiple purposes, including evaluating papers' soundness, interestingness, and clarity, the last two of which are subjective. In this work, we focus solely on evaluating soundness, the most important and objective criterion, and define the learning objective as jointly maximizing (1) precision, based on the soundness and substantiveness of review points, and (2) recall, the coverage of high-quality reference review points. Under this problem formulation, we construct a quality-labeled review point dataset, PaperSound-23K, using 1K ICLR submissions and 23K human-written and LLM-generated review points. Using this dataset, we train an AI reviewer model, PaperCritic, through reinforcement learning with the F1 score of precision and recall as the reward. Our experiments show that PaperCritic outperforms frontier models and models trained with other AI reviewer training methods on an in-domain test set. Surprisingly, learning to review ICLR submissions also transfers to reviewing Nature-family papers and even to out-of-domain reviewing tasks (e.g., code review, legal contract review, and feedback on story writing).

</details>

Abstract: Evaluating grammatical knowledge in language models requires benchmarks that cover diverse linguistic phenomena and are validated against human judgments. We introduce BLiMP-RO, a Romanian minimal-pair benchmark covering 50 grammatical phenomena across ten domains. It comprises 493 linguist-constructed sentence pairs, evaluated by native speakers in five experiments, and 5,942 additional semi-automatically generated, human-validated pairs. We further investigate grammatical generalization under limited linguistic input by training GPT-2 and GPT-BERT models from scratch on Romanian BabyBabelLM data and a newly collected corpus of 10 million words from child-targeted video captions. Increasing caption training data yields approximately 9 percentage points of accuracy improvement per tenfold increase in corpus size. At matched data sizes, BabyBabelLM generally outperforms video captions, while GPT-BERT consistently outperforms GPT-2. Our findings highlight how training data quantity, composition, and model architecture shape grammatical generalization in Romanian.

</details>

Abstract: We study encoder models that pick one of several options in a single forward pass, without generating text. This technical report compares recipes for fine-tuning UltraBERT-380M with one mask per option. The four recipes differ in how an option is scored and how the scores become probabilities; one follows the expert gate of DeepSeek-V3. Trained on the same data as Qwen3-0.6B with the LLM-as-Jev recipe (67.6% test accuracy), the encoder reaches 69.7% to 72.1%. Scoring an option by "yes" minus "no" instead of "yes" alone gives the larger gain, from 69.7% to 71.3% on the test set and from 57.8% to 59.6% on external benchmarks (56.1% for the decoder). Sigmoid normalisation adds 0.8 test points; a square-root softplus adds none. One request at a time on an NVIDIA A100, the encoder takes 29 ms per decision against 68 ms, but uses more peak memory (2.8 against 2.2 GiB). An auxiliary head, trained on human-marked evidence and on labels selected with a reward adapted from SelfCite, predicts in the same pass the sentences annotated as support for the answer.

</details>


### [172] [MineralExp: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Experimental Design in Real-World Mineral Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17931)
*Bing Wang,Mingyang Wang,Yuanhang Mi,Yue Xu,Kaibo Su,Li'ang Xiao,Chunguo Wu,Changchun Li,Ximing Li,Kun-Feng Qiu,Bo Yang*

Abstract: In mineral exploration, verifying geological hypotheses relies on designing a variety of geochemical experiments. While large language model (LLM) agents increasingly automate downstream geoscientific data analysis, a crucial upstream capability remains largely unexplored: automated geochemical experimental design, i.e., deciding which experiments to conduct before committing to costly laboratory work. Evaluating this capability of LLM agents is difficult for two reasons. First, geochemical experiments exhibit high methodological diversity and subjectivity: to test the same hypothesis, different geologists may choose different minerals and instruments, so no single protocol can serve as the reference answer. Second, open-ended experimental plans resist representation as structured labels, which makes objective scoring difficult. To address these challenges, we introduce MineralExp, a benchmark that reformulates geochemical experimental design as a hypothesis-based experiment selection problem, i.e., a multi-label classification problem. We abstract concrete instrument names and procedural details into a shared taxonomy of 19 experimental types across five mineral-system processes: Source, Transport, Deposition, Modification, and Preservation. This taxonomy yields deterministic multi-label targets while accommodating diverse experimental protocols. Constructed from published ore-deposit literature and verified by domain experts, MineralExp comprises 277 cases and 1,572 reference experimental plans. Given a geological hypothesis and initial context, an LLM agent selects the relevant tasks and generates concrete experimental plans, which are scored by multi-label classification metrics and an LLM-as-a-Judge metric. The experimental results show that current agents generally achieve high recall but low precision, tending to propose more experiments than are necessary to test the hypothesis.

</details>


### [173] [Can You Trust Your Finance Agent? Drift, Diagnosis and Auditable Intervention on One Shipped Financial Research Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17939)

Abstract: Can you trust a finance agent you did not build? We take the reference agent shipped with the Vals Finance Agent Benchmark v2, leave it untuned, and ask four questions a leaderboard score does not answer. Is the score stable? Seven identical runs on 18 questions pass between 105 and 123 of 149 rubric criteria, and the bill varies by up to 17%. When the agent fails, who owns the fix? We classify 20 failed trajectories, from three runs of nine held-out questions, by the layer whose owner would remedy them, under a taxonomy frozen in advance: 13 belong to the agent's instructions, 3 to its tools, 4 to the model, none to its data. Can that diagnosis be trusted? Four blind readers from three model families, given only the traces, name the same fault as our LLM analyzer in 94% of cases, the rate at which they agree with one another (93%). Can a change be trusted? An optimization loop reads the traces, makes one targeted change per round and keeps it only if it clears a fixed threshold. Under a cost objective its final configuration costs 0.34x the baseline on the held-out questions (95% CI [0.22, 0.58]) at accuracy within noise. Under an accuracy objective it gains 14 points on the development questions and 1.5 on the held-out ones (CI [-5.3, +7.2]). Judged against the measured run-to-run noise, under a rule written before the test was opened, the first is a real saving and the second is not. This is a case study of one agent and nine test questions, not a general result.

</details>


### [174] [In the Eye of the Beholder: Human Label Variation in Textual and Visual Entailment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17954)

Abstract: Human Label Variation (HLV) is increasingly recognised as an informative signal that reflects meaningful differences in how people interpret the same input. While HLV has been studied extensively in Natural Language Inference (NLI), much less is known about HLV in Visual Entailment (VE; Xie et al., 2019), where the textual premise is replaced by an image, keeping the hypothesis and label space fixed. We introduce VariNLI-VE, a dataset for studying how HLV changes between paired NLI and VE instances. We re-annotate a subset of originally neutral SNLI (Bowman et al., 2015) instances and their visual counterparts, with the same annotators evaluating both the textual and visual versions. Each annotation includes a label, confidence score, and explanation, yielding 2,250 annotations. Labels and explanations are collected jointly, providing ecologically valid explanations. Because the image may reveal information not stated in the text, one might expect disagreement to decrease in VE. Instead, we find no clear overall reduction: disagreement decreases for some instances while it increases for others. Explanations reveal further variation, as annotators can retain the same label while changing their rationale. When evaluating vision-language models (VLMs) on the paired data, we find lower accuracy, weaker alignment with human label distributions, and generally higher uncertainty in VE than in NLI.

</details>


### [175] [Toward Universal Translatability: On the Statistical Ontology of Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17963)
*Katia Schwerzmann*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: This paper argues that the problem of translation functions both as a point of origin and as a normative horizon for contemporary generative AI. In current discourse around ''world models,'' their attainment is framed as the achievement of internal consistency, cross-modal alignment, and physical grounding. Taken together, these demands converge on an ideal of the model as a universal translation machine capable of establishing lossless correspondences across linguistic, visual, and physical representation spaces. I show that this ideal presupposes a metaphysical commitment that I call a statistical ontology: the belief that reality has an underlying, computable statistical structure that can be recovered through sufficient data and modeling, rather than being shaped and accessed through situated human interpretation and labor. Tracing this commitment back to Warren Weaver's 1949 Memorandum on machine translation, I outline how translation was historically recast as code deciphering and, later, as the alignment of probability distributions across structured outputs. Finally, I contrast this paradigm with approaches in Translation Studies that foreground opacity, power asymmetries, and the translator's positionality. I argue that generative AI's statistical ontology is pre-Kantian, pre-critical and obscures the infrastructures and human labor that make machine translatability---as alignment---possible in the first place.

</details>

Abstract: When a conversational Voice AI agent places an outbound call, the far end may be a live person, a voicemail greeting, an interactive voice response (IVR) system, or increasingly another AI system answering on the callee's behalf. Standard Answering Machine Detection (AMD) reports only whether a human or a machine picked up, a design meant for human call centres, where one machine is as uninteresting as another. That single bit does not tell a Voice AI agent what to do, since leaving a voicemail message, navigating an IVR and holding a short exchange with another AI are three different actions and a two-valued label cannot distinguish them. In 1,000 randomly sampled calls from our own outbound deployment, a live person answered 48.1%, a voicemail system 44.6%, an AI 6.9% and an IVR 0.4%. Our proposed framework, FEND, reads the far end's audio and its streaming transcript and runs one detector for each automated class (voicemail, IVR, AI callee), with human the default when nothing fires, returning a single label within 20 seconds. On a public benchmark the voicemail components reach 100% accuracy without ever misclassifying a live human, where Asterisk's AMD misclassifies 30 to 66% of them. For the AI callee, acoustic features do not transfer across recording conditions, but speech timing does, separating AI from human at 0.952 ROC-AUC, because synthesised speech leaves far shorter gaps between words. On production traffic, separating a live person from any automated far end reaches F1 0.979, and only 2.9% of live people are given an automated label, the error that matters most for an agent acting on the result.

</details>


### [177] [Agent-as-a-Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18067)

Abstract: Artificial intelligence has progressed from task-specific systems toward general models spanning perception, generation, reasoning, and autonomous interaction, making reliable evaluation essential for comparison, deployment, and post-training feedback. At scale, modern evaluation pipelines increasingly rely on LLM-as-a-Judge to produce scores, preferences, and critiques; in its fixed-context setting, the judge relies on the supplied evaluand and evaluation materials. Yet a reliable verdict may require evidence beyond the supplied evaluand and context---either because critical environmental states remain unrecorded in the static execution log, or because controversial factual claims require external verification. Passive judges cannot obtain that missing evidence before deciding, especially when evaluating agent trajectories, executable artifacts, or domain-specific reports. Recent work therefore develops active evaluators that seek missing evidence and update their grounds for judgment before deciding. We bring these advances together through Agent-as-a-Judge, focusing on how evaluators construct and control checking processes within the broader LLM-based judging landscape. We define the scope through evaluation-directed checks and the use of their results, covering both structured workflows and observation-driven control. We organize the design space by multi-agent collaboration, tool integration, memory, and planning, and show how these mechanisms supply the checks and evidence on which a verdict rests. We consolidate published scores and reproduce missing entries for key agentic judges; the comparison reveals uneven agentic gains and motivates dedicated agentic evaluation. We conclude by characterizing application demands and open challenges in cost, reliability, safety, and governance that condition trusted deployment of agentic judges at scale.

</details>


### [178] [VEPO: Mitigating Multilingual Hallucinations in LVLMs via Visual Evidence-guided Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18096)
*Chengpeng Fu,Xiaocheng Feng,Wenshuai Huo,Yichong Huang,Baohang Li,Duyu Tang,Ting Liu*

Abstract: Large vision-language models (LVLMs) achieve strong multimodal performance, yet they remain prone to object hallucination---generating answers that are linguistically fluent but visually ungrounded. This issue is especially severe in low-resource languages, where weaker cross-modal alignment causes models to rely more on language priors than on image evidence. Existing remedies either require costly multilingual annotations or external translation/reward models, or intervene at inference time in ways that can distort the output language. We propose VEPO(Visual Evidence-guided Preference Optimization), a training-time method that transfers reliable visual grounding from English to other languages without external models. VEPO first localizes key visual evidence using English queries via mid-layer cross-modal attention, then constructs preference pairs that share the same correct answer but differ in evidence sufficiency (full image vs.\ key-region masked image). Direct preference optimization on these pairs encourages low-resource inputs to prefer answers supported by complete visual evidence. Experiments on multilingual POPE and MME with Chinese, French, and Hindi show consistent hallucination reduction and perception gains, with the largest improvements on the lowest-resource language. Analyses further confirm that VEPO sharpens question-relevant attention and that evidence-guided masking outperforms random masking.

</details>


### [179] [Contagious Fingerprints: When LLMs Inherit Another Model's Identity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18104)
*Zailong Tian*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: 解析错误

Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: The fingerprint by which a classifier recognizes the LLM behind a text is usually treated as a fixed property of the model. We show that it is contagious: a model that continues a conversation begun by another model inherits that model's fingerprint. This undermines audits of API relays, which can serve the first turns of a conversation with the model a customer pays for and silently hand the rest to a cheaper one. After such a handoff, detection of the substitute by a text classifier drops from 0.97 to 0.72 (DeepSeek replacing Claude Sonnet) and from 0.87 to 0.54 for a same-vendor downgrade (Haiku), and retraining on handoff data repairs only substitutes seen in training. Preregistered experiments trace the contagion to imitation of the history's style. History also moves a model away from its own fingerprint: active probes are not camouflaged, but when calibrated on fresh conversations they flag \emph{honest} models on long contexts at rates up to 1.00, and a standard MMD equality test rejects all nine models we test. Both failures come from comparing a history-dependent reply with a history-free reference. We propose \textsc{Lineup}, which samples references from the claimed model \emph{on the same history} and tests the audited reply by its rank among them. \textsc{Lineup} controls false positives by exchangeability and needs no fitted threshold or log-probabilities. With contemporaneous references on two closed models, its false-positive rate is 0.056 (preregistered bound 0.10), and it detects 0.81 of substitute sessions across six pairs after four audited turns.

</details>


### [180] [Folding Language Models into Themselves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18113)

Abstract: Knowledge distillation usually trains a compact student from scratch, which needs a large amount of data even though the teacher's weights already encode most of what the student has to learn. In this work, we explore building the student directly from the teacher's weights. We propose Concertina, which folds every group of N adjacent teacher layers into one shared block that is executed N times. Each loop step carries a low-rank correction initialized from the layer it replaces, and a per-token gate mixes these corrections, to let every token recover the behavior of the original layers from a single shared block. We further design a distillation data recipe that balances general, mathematical, academic and targeted multiple-choice data, to recover all capabilities of the teacher together rather than one at the expense of another. On Qwen3-4B, a 2.27B Concertina model distilled on 22B tokens retains about 90% of the teacher on commonsense and mathematical reasoning and 84% on average across eight benchmarks. It outperforms same-size dense and layer-sharing baselines, as well as Qwen3-1.7B, which is trained on over a thousand times more data. These results suggest that a compact model need not be retrained at scale, since folding the teacher into itself and distilling it on the right data recovers most of its capabilities. Code is available at https://github.com/myboker/concertina.

</details>

Abstract: The automated transformation of unstructured natural language process descriptions into Business Process Model and Notation (BPMN) diagrams remains a challenging task, due to linguistic ambiguity and the difficulty to detect non-adjacent control-flow relationships. This paper proposes a solution for generating BPMN models from natural language descriptions through a multi-layered pipeline that integrates transformer-based architectures, Large Language Models and ontology population. The proposed method starts with natural language descriptions and separates textual processing, control-flow relation extraction, and structural validation into dedicated components. Entity extraction is performed using a fine-tuned RoBERTa-based transformer model while a control-flow engine captures non-adjacent relationships through LLM-based inference. To improve generalization under limited available data, the approach uses a dynamic few-shot strategy with con- trolled template input construction, enabling improved generalization under limited data availability. Additionally, an ontology population mechanism is introduced to represent the generated process models as semantic knowledge structures. For evaluation purposes we have used from the literature and industry reference processes. Additionally, we have validated the proposed method on a real-world heart disease patients transitional care process, capturing practical constraints and multi-actor collaboration, and serving as an expert-defined benchmark scenario. Experimental results demonstrate strong performance in the relation extraction task, achieving an accuracy of 90.7%, as well as maintaining structural alignment between the generated and reference BPMN models across multiple evaluation scenarios scenarios.

</details>

Abstract: We present NER-D, a cross-domain benchmark for German NER comprising 13 datasets spanning 9 domains, including General/Politics, Wikipedia, Legal, Medical, Historical, Financial, Agriculture and Archaeology. We examine the performance of both proprietary LLMs and state-of-the-art small open-source models using normal prompting and further examine the performance gains achievable via in-context learning and LoRA fine-tuning. Our results show that without any sort of optimization a proprietary model (GPT-5.4 mini) outperforms the smaller open-source models on 6 out of 13 datasets, but LoRA fine-tuning enables the smaller models to substantially outperform the proprietary baseline. We further analyze the influence of the domain and the tagset used in each task on the performance achieved in the different evaluation settings. Our benchmark will be available as a resource for the development of future German NER methods on both general and specialized domains.\footnote{We make our code, data, and models publicly available upon acceptance of this paper.

Abstract: Answer continuation often returns a diagnosis already stated in unfinished clinical reasoning. We study this behavior in MamaReason, adapted from dense Gemma 4 31B, on MamaBench's 217 paired maternal and child-health presentations. An explicit-commitment extractor identifies a diagnosis in 75 of 94 failed RL first passes and 75 of 93 failed SFT first passes. The continuation matches that diagnosis in 67/75 RL cases and 58/75 SFT cases, using diagnosis strings alone. Reading these commitments without additional generation recovers 30 of the 40 RL pairs restored by continuation under the archived correctness labels. Recurrence controls distinguish routine mentions from repeated answer selection. An offline second-commitment trigger measures the tradeoff between earlier readout and paired accuracy. The resulting decomposition separates diagnostic content already present in a trace, completion of an accepted answer, and paired diagnostic correctness. For clinical language-model evaluation, these are distinct outcomes that become visible when the same reasoning trace is examined through multiple readouts.

</details>

Abstract: Whether a language model's answer is correct often depends on context: the same value can be right under one jurisdiction, date, or legal definition and wrong under another. We introduce GT-FENG (General Theory of Factual Evaluation and Normative Grounding), the first general framework to unify contextual/temporal correctness and rule-based error attribution across law, policy, and other rule-governed domains. GT-FENG checks answers against the applicable rules and identifies documented rules that match incorrect answers. It shows that when multiple rules give the same value, the answer alone cannot tell which rule the model used. In Medicaid income-limit answers from two models, documenting the asked state's own records reduces the number of wrong answers that match only other states' values by 68-78%, and more than half of stable wrong answers then match two or more documented sources. GT-FENG provides a foundation for evaluating language models in rule-governed domains and for future benchmarks and evaluation toolkits. We release GT-FENG as an open-source Python package, gtfeng.

</details>

Abstract: Detecting an intertextual reference is only the first step; philologists also need to know how a later author reworked the source. We explain a reuse pair word by word: each word of the reusing passage is first aligned to its source word, then labeled with the operation that transformed it, such as a copy, a change of inflection, or a substitution. Such word-level operations are expensive to annotate, and only a few corpora annotated at this level exist for text reuse in Latin literature. We describe a procedure that needs only a limited number of annotations: synthetic pairs, built by applying known operations to Latin text, train a first model, and active learning chooses the pairs an annotator corrects. Among several architectures, a model that links and labels together performs best: a pointer network that, for every reuse word, chooses a source word and names the operation in the same step. We show that with active learning, 200 pairs chosen by uncertainty are enough for the model to match one trained on every annotated pair of the 1,490 references of the Loci Similes benchmark. We release our code, word-level predictions for all references, and an interactive demo.

</details>

Participants were either informed that some of the material was AI-generated or received no information about its origin. AI attribution did not affect reaction times or agreement judgments. This result suggests that AI-generated content does not automatically raise credibility concerns and may be evaluated with less scrutiny compared to human-produced content.

Abstract: We introduce Gamer, a generalist agent framework built for long-horizon tasks in complex interactive environments. Its end-to-end architecture combines perception, memory management, and multi-step reasoning. A shared training and deployment pipeline supports interaction with both conventional 2D graphical interfaces and complex 3D simulated worlds. We first continue pre-training on a dataset constructed from tens of thousands of hours of device-level keyboard and mouse interaction. These trajectories span simulated games and embodied environments, allowing the model to learn environmental dynamics across both physical and virtual domains. Sparse-thinking instruction tuning then substantially reduces redundant computation while preserving the reasoning needed for complex decisions. We train the policy for long-horizon interaction with a stable multi-turn reinforcement learning method that addresses finite context windows, accumulated rollout errors, and sparse rewards. For real-time interaction, a cloud-edge inference engine serves numerous simulated game environments concurrently, supports unbounded-context execution, and keeps online inference latency low. Across multiple challenging 3D open-world games, Gamer demonstrates solid generalization and operational efficiency comparable to skilled players. It also achieves competitive results on a range of conventional 2D game benchmarks. These results suggest that even repetitive keyboard and mouse trajectories can support substantial gains from scaling data and learning algorithms. Training on these trajectories helps Gamer learn complex skills more steadily in environments with both physical and digital properties. This approach offers a path toward generalist agents that can interact across digital and physical worlds.

</details>

Abstract: When model predictions track an unfolding human affect trajectory, alignment may reflect narrative position, affective words, or text-specific reader-response dynamics. We introduce LitArc-Bench to separate these explanations: 39 Chinese-language literary excerpts segmented into seven checkpoints, with 430 questionnaires from 81 adults in two classroom groups (9,020 VAD ratings). We compare three future-blind model runs with leave-one-text-out position priors, lexical affect, and human-human references. Position alone reaches r = .470 for arousal, making raw alignment insufficient. Yet all six V/A transition advantages over a joint position-lexicon control have text-bootstrap intervals above zero. Cross-fitted models match the correct human text better than 38 mismatched texts in all nine model-dimension cells (p < .0001); a text-invariant negative control returns chance (p = 1). On human transitions at least 0.5 points against the position trend, models recover 69-76% of valence directions and 53-68% for arousal and dominance. Under crossed participant-text resampling, all nine identity intervals remain above zero; joint-control advantages are robust for valence and directional but less certain for arousal. High shape agreement still coexists with scale bias. LitArc-Bench identifies which components of literary reader response are recoverable beyond shallow explanations and why shape, calibration, and human variability require separate evaluation.

</details>


### [189] [Do Verifier Judgments Survive Abstraction? Measuring Verification Transport for LLM Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18471)

Abstract: LLM-based verifiers are increasingly used to evaluate agent executions, but their reliability is typically measured under a single representation of the trajectory. In deployed agent systems, the same execution may be exposed through composed tools, delegation boundaries, or compacted histories, changing what execution evidence is directly observable to the verifier. It therefore remains unclear whether correct verifier judgments persist across such abstraction boundaries. We introduce Verification Transport, a reliability test that measures judgment stability under abstraction-induced changes in execution observability. We construct matched native, abstracted, and restored views of the same frozen executions while holding the task, realized trajectory, outcome, ground truth, evaluation criterion, and verifier fixed. Across four agent-evaluation benchmarks, seven verifier backbones, four verification protocols, and three abstraction boundaries, we find substantial transport failure, reaching about 31% under macro abstraction and delegation. Verification protocols exhibit distinct failure modes: direct verification more often produces incorrect verdicts, whereas evidence-grounded and deliberative protocols tend to abstain under limited observability. This suggests that lower transport failure rates do not necessarily imply greater reliability. Restoring the corresponding execution provenance recovers 85.5% of transport failures, while recovery is lower when provenance is lossy or unavailable. These results show that native-view accuracy alone is insufficient to characterize verifier reliability when execution evidence crosses abstraction boundaries.

</details>


### [190] [Interactive Program Induction with Language Model Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18485)
*Robin Shing Moon Chan,Ryan Cotterell*

TL;DR: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Program induction refers to the process in which a learner aims to recover a program from a few given input-output examples. This paradigm broadens programming access by letting users demonstrate the rule-based behavior they want to apply to unseen data, instead of having to implement it themselves. However, recovering a program from finite input-output examples is fundamentally ambiguous, as a finite set of input-output examples is theoretically consistent with infinitely many programs whose output diverges on unseen inputs. We view this problem from a Bayesian perspective, where, given a prior $p^0$ over programs, identifying the target program means concentrating posterior probability mass on it by observing additional input-output examples. In the interactive program induction setting, the learner chooses maximally informative inputs and queries an oracle, such as the user, for their outputs. We derive a greedy query-selection algorithm and show that it terminates after $\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}γ\log 1/p^0(f^\star))$ informative oracle queries, and returns the target program $f^\star$ up to approximate equivalence when no other class outweighs it under the prior. The algorithm uses a language model as the prior under a PAC-style relaxation of program equivalence, where the query-selection objective is estimated using importance sampling. On the CodeARC benchmark, the algorithm improves accuracy over the LM only agent by up to 22 percentage points while observing up to 10.5 fewer input-output examples.

</details>

Abstract: Common data elements (CDEs) support clinical data comparability but are usually defined manually. We investigated whether large language model agents could derive a CDE schema from sarcoma case report abstracts and apply it at scale. An agent proposed 72,909 candidate CDEs from 24,588 PubMed abstracts; agentic consolidation yielded 1,570 CDEs. We retained 53 supported by at least 50 abstracts and attempted evidence-linked extraction across the corpus. Processing succeeded for all retained CDEs in 79.2% of abstracts, including responses indicating non-mention. Two human reviewers approved 83.0% of retained CDEs for relevance, 67.9% for clarity, and 56.6% for correctness. In 100 sampled abstracts, exact agreement with the value extracted by the LLM was 94.9% among 1,116 pairs with a non-empty answer (abstract-level bootstrap 95% CI, 93.0-96.4%). Errors concentrated in anatomical and temporal fields. These findings support agent-assisted schema drafting followed by expert curation before large-scale extraction.

Abstract: Verifiers provide important reward signals for reinforcement learning of large language models (LLMs). However, it is challenging to develop or create reliable verifiers, especially for code generation tasks. A well-disguised wrong solution program may only be detected by carefully human-written edge cases that are difficult to synthesize automatically. To address this issue, we propose HARDTESTGEN, an approach to synthesize high-quality test cases for algorithmic coding problems. We curate a comprehensive algorithmic programming dataset HARDTESTS with 26.6k problems and high-quality synthetic tests. Compared with existing tests, HARDTESTGEN tests demonstrate significantly higher accuracy in verifying LLM-generated code (+11.22 percentage points in precision, the percentage of actually correct code within the predicted correct ones). We also show that downstream post-training -- including rejection sampling and reinforcement learning (RL) -- using HARDTESTS verifier results in improved performance of LLM code generation. We open-source our dataset and synthesis pipeline at https://leililab.github.io/HardTests/.

</details>

### [193] [Agentic-Teacher: An Agentic Data Engine for Failure-Driven VLA Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17394)

Abstract: Improving a pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) model requires demonstrations that address its weaknesses and provide effective supervision for learning. Coding agents can autonomously generate demonstrations through environment interaction, yet successful execution alone does not ensure effective supervision for VLA learning. We introduce Agentic-Teacher, an autonomous data engine for failure-driven VLA post-training. Given a VLA checkpoint, a task specification, and configured environment tools, the engine analyzes failed rollouts, selects intervention states, and iteratively develops closed-loop code policies through execution feedback. Validated policies become reusable demonstration generators that collect successful continuations from states where the VLA struggles. To make these demonstrations suitable for learning, Agentic-Teacher introduces temporally correlated action noise to broaden state-action coverage, removes idle frames while preserving gripper transitions, and filters trajectories using the frozen base model's flow-matching loss. The resulting demonstrations are combined with successful VLA rollouts for post-training, yielding an updated policy that operates without coding-agent assistance. Agentic-Teacher thus connects the weaknesses of a target VLA to both the generation and curation of its training data. In simulation, it improves $π_0$ success rates from 79.4% to 87.8% on LIBERO-10, from 80.4% to 90.6% on MetaWorld-10, and from 0% to 30.0% across three RoboCasa tasks.

</details>

Abstract: A rescue robot entering a building with thirty seconds on the clock should sprint toward the single most promising room; given ten minutes, it should instead explore a broader tour. Yet vision-language-navigation (VLN) models are typically evaluated under a fixed episode limit that the policy never observes, even though time-critical missions require behavior to adapt to the available budget. We study budget-conditioned object search: given a target category and an explicit action budget, an agent must find as many distinct target instances as possible before the budget expires. We investigate how to supervise VLNs for this task by comparing a privileged TSP expert, which plans near-optimal routes using the full map and true target locations, with a room-level Bellman expert that maximizes expected return under partial observability using high-level cross-scene semantic priors. Despite the TSP expert achieving substantially higher expert performance, its demonstrations are less effective for learning exploration: on held-out HM3D scenes, the Bellman-supervised VLN consistently outperforms the TSP-supervised VLN, with the advantage becoming more pronounced at longer budgets. At 2000 steps (500m), the Bellman-supervised VLN finds 71.4% of reachable target instances, compared with 65.5% for the TSP-supervised VLN and 44.8% for the pretrained VLN. These results highlight a distinction between optimal routing and optimal exploration: effective exploration supervision should optimize decisions under partial observability rather than imitate routes that are optimal only once the environment is fully known.

</details>

Abstract: Image-goal planning with a frozen visual world model depends on how predicted observations are scored against the goal. Mean-squared error (MSE) between embeddings from a pretrained visual encoder can provide a useful signal near a goal, yet favor states across an obstacle that are visually similar to the goal and difficult to reach. We fit a small residual adapter on top of frozen I-JEPA embeddings to improve this latent goal cost. Starting near each goal, a planner collects trajectories from progressively wider regions of initial states. The adapter learns from the order of observations along successful trajectories, with additional comparisons inferred from failed rollouts. No task-specific progress labels are used for fitting, so the procedure can be applied to other goal-reaching tasks. DotWall and PointMaze are used in this work. In paired 50-case evaluations with identical starts, goals, world models, and planner settings, success increases from 54% to 98% in DotWall and from 82% to 96% in PointMaze. On 16 unseen DotWall layout-goal cases, the adapter fitted on one fixed layout improves agreement between latent-cost ordering and shortest legal-path ordering from 71.9% to 85.8%. The results indicate that planning experience can improve a frozen visual representation's goal cost without retraining the encoder or the world model.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-turn conversational human-robot interaction (HRI) continues to be a challenging problem in robotics, as many existing systems rely on rigid, command-driven interfaces that can be unintuitive for users. Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) provide a promising direction, given their ability to process natural language, generate structured outputs, and support tool-based reasoning, all of which are relevant for interpreting user intent in robotic settings. However, progress in this area is constrained by the limited availability of datasets and benchmarks that capture the nuances of real-world human communication, such as multi-turn dialogue, ambiguity, and context-dependent requests.
  In this work, we present a synthetically generated multi-turn dialogue dataset for HRI, annotated with action intents, object references, and task plans across a range of scenarios and user personas. Our primary focus is on evaluating the capability of small, open-weight language models for robotic tasks, particularly in settings where local deployment and efficiency are important. To this end, we frame intent extraction as a tool-calling problem where each predicted action corresponds to a structured function call with arguments grounded in the dialogue and benchmark models ranging from 270M to 8B parameters on their ability to perform intent extraction and action planning without task-specific fine-tuning.
  Our results show that these models can achieve reasonable performance on the task even at relatively small scales and without any task-specific fine-tuning. This suggests that compact, locally deployable models already provide a useful baseline for conversational robotic systems. We expect that task-specific fine-tuning and more realistic training data will enhance performance further and make such models increasingly suitable for real-world robotic applications.

</details>

Abstract: Safety in robotics does not solely depend on geometric constraints, but also on semantics: the given task, environment properties, and interactions between objects in the scene. We present a framework based on vision-language models (VLMs) to infer task- and scene-dependent safety constraints for robot manipulation. We provide the VLM a structured safety tool based on an RGB-D observation and a natural-language task instruction that first decides whether the task should be rejected since its execution may result in a non-recoverable hazardous outcome. Otherwise, it identifies preventable object-pair hazards and specifies the spatial relations that must be avoided. Open-vocabulary segmentation and depth information are then used to ground the detected objects in 3D and construct continuous risk fields that represent the corresponding hazardous regions. These fields are integrated with an obstacle avoidance module that corrects nominal robot motions during execution while accounting for the risk field. We evaluate the framework on preventable hazards, tasks requiring rejection, and safe variants in simulation and the real-world. Project website: https://vlm-hazard-riskfield.github.io

</details>

Abstract: Robots with weak motors must ride their natural dynamics rather than fight them. We swing up a direct-drive planar two-link robot, whose motors deliver only about 6\,\% of its shoulder gravity torque, along its nonlinear normal modes, two-dimensional invariant manifolds consisting of periodic orbits. On a non-strict mode, injecting energy necessarily moves the state off the manifold, so pumping and stabilization compete for the same bounded torque. We split the input torque into a power-neutral steering component and a scalar pumping input, the only component that changes the energy. The allocation then becomes a linear program in two variables: maximize pumping subject to the torque limits and one control-Lyapunov constraint. The constraint uses the model-based rate of the error, so no stabilizing feedback gain is needed. Experiments in hardware show swing-ups along both modes and compare the allocation with summing-and-clipping and with prioritized stabilization.

</details>

Abstract: Long-horizon navigation from indoors to outdoors remains an open problem for humanoid robots: one instruction covers rooms, doors and roads, and the camera moves with the gait. Worken, AGIBOT's measure of productive robot work, depends on robots that reach their workplaces autonomously. We present X-Nav, to our knowledge the first system to take a bipedal humanoid through a language-instructed long-horizon mission from indoors to outdoors. X-Nav covers a pipeline from data to deployment. Its data engine collects navigation data in open-source scenes, in digital twins of deployment sites and on real robots. Its navigation model, MELIOR, takes only RGB images from the robot's built-in camera and comes in two versions: MELIOR-A predicts actions, and MELIOR-N predicts a navigation goal. Its agentic workflow, X-Navarch, turns a brief human instruction into sub-tasks and deploys MELIOR on the robot with explicit target navigation and obstacle avoidance. On the Lingxi X2, X-Nav completes a mission of about a hundred metres from an office lobby to a store in a neighbouring building; LiDAR and odometry are used for target approach and low-level control. The mission is completed with both versions of MELIOR: the robot follows a change of destination and walks through a door from the lobby to the road, and with X-Navarch it also passes static and dynamic obstacles on the route. During deployment, MELIOR is adapted with data collected in a digital twin of the site; the adaptation raises its closed-loop success rate in the twin, and the adapted model is then deployed on the robot at the real site. In addition, MELIOR achieves competitive results on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: Human demonstrations are becoming the main source of data for dexterous manipulation, and real-to-sim-to-real pipelines multiply each demonstration in simulation. These pipelines rebuild the interaction from vision, which recovers how the hand moves. Which fingers touch, when, and how hard remain unobserved, because a hand pose shows where each finger stops on the surface rather than how far it is driven to close. This gap is a force gap: on held-out recordings, a hand policy that imitates the observed pose grips with 0.31x the recorded force, and a policy trained on physics replays of vision-tracked poses grips with 1.55x. TouchTwin closes the gap with measured human touch. Each selected TouchScale recording, captured with head and wrist RGB-D cameras and an 880-taxel glove, is rebuilt in MuJoCo. Coding agents write the objects as physics-tested programs, and a per-subject tactile hand replays the interaction with the recorded touch setting the contact and the closing force of each finger. In simulation, a policy trained on touch-grounded replays grips at 0.99x the recorded force and succeeds in 20/20 trials on held-out recordings and 35/36 unseen perturbations; a contact predictor trained on them misses 13% of real contact frames, against 32% for vision-based replays. Each replay also seeds perturbed demonstrations with simulated contact and force labels, rendered as realistic video in new views and scenes.

</details>


### [201] [On the Utility of Pre-Trained Visual Representations for Policy Learning of Contact-Rich Insertion Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17631)

Abstract: Visuomotor imitation learning has become a predominant paradigm for real-world robotic manipulation, yet it remains data-inefficient and brittle to task-irrelevant visual shifts. Pre-trained visual representations promise to ease this burden by distilling priors from internet-scale data, but the ``right'' representation for robotics remains unresolved: existing studies span broad but sparse task distributions and reach conclusions that vary and even conflict, pointing to strong task and setting dependence. We examine this question by bringing a magnifying glass to a common contact-rich manipulation task: insertion. We compare representations from widely used vision foundation models against an explicit contact-centric representation on two closely related insertion tasks, perturb each along controlled axes of occlusion and physical tolerance, and test whether conclusions transfer to the real world. Task dependence proves to be more subtle than previous, broadly scoped evaluations revealed: the best representation differs even between our near-identical tasks, and small changes shift trends in unpredictable ways. Despite this variability, results across simulation and real-world experiments demonstrate the distinct value of spatially-grounded, and in particular, contact-centric information for contact-rich policy learning, while underscoring how difficult it is to estimate such cues reliably under realistic visual complexity and sensor noise. We hope these findings motivate further study of representations in contact-rich manipulation, along with advances in contact perception and its integration into visuomotor policy learning.

</details>

Abstract: Personalized bioelectronic devices for wound therapy require not only conformal fabrication on complex body surfaces but also functional adaptation of their electrical characteristics to individual wound geometries. Here, we present an AI-guided robotic in situ printing platform for fabricating wireless wound electrotherapy devices directly on transparent wound dressings. The system integrates a 3D vision camera, a six-degree-of-freedom robotic arm, direct-ink-writing, and geometry-aware RF design optimization. AI-based wound segmentation is used to determine wound dimensions and local surface curvature, while the reconstructed three-dimensional geometry is incorporated into the electromagnetic design of the receiving coil and interdigitated electrodes. In contrast to conventional approaches that use surface geometry only for trajectory generation, the proposed method accounts for both anatomical curvature and tissue dielectric loading to compensate for geometry-induced shifts in resonant behavior. A printable silver ink was characterized in terms of rheology, dimensional reproducibility, electrical resistance, and electromechanical stability, and was used to fabricate multilayer wireless devices directly on curved polyurethane dressings. Wireless power transfer, impedance characteristics, and electric-field delivery were evaluated experimentally and numerically. Finally, robotic in situ fabrication was demonstrated on an anthropomorphic tissue-equivalent forearm phantom, where the measured dielectric properties of the phantom were incorporated into the RF optimization, enabling resonant operation near the target frequency of 25 MHz. This work establishes a perception-to-design-to-fabrication framework for geometry-adaptive wireless bioelectronics and provides a technological foundation for future physical-AI-enabled manufacturing of personalized on-body electrotherapy devices.

</details>


### [203] [Imitation Policies Learn a Proprioceptive Anchor: A Predictive Law for Perception Noise and Its Closed Loop Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17658)

Abstract: Imitation policies for robot manipulation are often trained on noisy object positions, and they learn to discount the noisy reading. We show how much they discount it, and why. Because demonstrators steer toward the object, the robot's own state carries information about where the object is. A trained policy combines this proprioceptive anchor with the reading and weights the reading by $α= V/(V + s^2)$, where $s^2$ is the reading noise and $V$ the anchor's error variance. $V$ belongs to the learner and is far smaller than a linear model of the demonstrations implies, which is why classical errors-in-variables (EIV) regression overestimates $α$. Measured once, $V$ predicted ten held-out sensitivities across two robomimic tasks, two learners and four new noise levels, with mean absolute error 0.025 against 0.37 for classical EIV, and a parameter-free bias test rules out the simpler explanation that the policies just overestimate the noise. The discount does not carry over to deployment, where the policy produces its own state: closed-loop sensitivity to a persistent reading offset exceeded $α$ in all 15 policies that passed the registered gates, although the stricter registered prediction, that all would follow the offset fully, held for 8 of them. Post hoc, lower $α$ went with lower success, steeply for diffusion, and a registered prospective test confirmed this for diffusion on Can at two new noise levels: the measured $α$ predicted success before any rollout, within 0.03 (95% intervals $\pm 0.08$), although training noise predicts it equally well. Every test was registered before its data, including the classical benchmark that failed, and all other analyses are labelled post hoc; all results are in simulation with synthetic Gaussian noise.

</details>

Abstract: The development of intelligent chassis increasingly requires the integration of environmental and perception information with high-fidelity vehicle dynamics to support environment-aware intelligent chassis control. However, existing autonomous-driving simulators and professional vehicle-dynamics tools are generally developed for different purposes, making it difficult to integrate environmental information, vehicle dynamics, and chassis algorithms. This paper proposes EViDS, an Environment-Vehicle Dynamics Integrated Simulation Platform for Intelligent Chassis Development. EViDS integrates CARLA-based environment and sensor simulation with CarSim-based high-fidelity vehicle dynamics. Reference-pose-based state alignment and multi-rate interaction maintain spatial and temporal consistency, while ray-casting-based tire-road interaction transfers environmental information to the vehicle-dynamics model. Scenario configuration, synchronized data acquisition, and algorithm interfaces further support intelligent chassis development. The platform is evaluated through environment-dynamics interaction tests with vehicle-dynamics consistency comparisons, together with intelligent chassis applications. Results show that EViDS preserves vehicle-dynamics characteristics, reliably transfers environmental information, and supports online closed-loop intelligent chassis control, providing a unified simulation basis for environment-aware intelligent chassis development and validation.

</details>

Abstract: End-to-end autonomous driving is predominantly evaluated through ego-vehicle safety and task completion. Its deployment in urban traffic, however, creates sustained interactions among vehicles whose collective effects remain insufficiently evaluated. We introduce Ego2Flow, a closed-loop benchmark for assessing the traffic-system impact of autonomous driving on maps distilled from real-world cities. To construct realistic 3D urban environments for sustained closed-loop traffic simulation, we develop an agent-guided map distillation framework that integrates real-world maps and observations with complementary geographic information. Through an iterative feedback mechanism and a traffic information graph that jointly guide scene generation, the framework preserves lane topology, critical road geometry and decision-relevant visual conditions while simplifying nonessential details and completing unobserved regions. Using our proposed framework, we generate and publicly release six CARLA maps from the Argoverse 2 dataset, covering the corresponding real-world regions and enabling paired comparisons of driving behavior under real and simulated observations. On these maps, Ego2Flow evaluates how autonomous driving policies affect traffic efficiency, safety, stability and energy consumption across diverse road structures. Compared with existing construction methods, our framework achieves the lowest average displacement error between planning outputs from paired real and simulated observations. We further benchmark representative driving policies to characterize their system-level impact on urban traffic. Project Page: https://github.com/Fisher-007/Ego2Flow.

</details>

Abstract: Depth and 3D foundation models can predict geometry from RGB images, allowing zero-shot object navigation without a depth sensor. However, when predicted depth is mapped as if it came from a sensor, view-dependent errors accumulate in the map, remove exploration goals, and end the search before the agent can observe and correct them. We propose ERA, a training-free object navigation system that replaces the depth sensor with depth predicted from RGB images and poses. ERA follows a paradigm we call Move with Error: movement provides new views that correct map errors, and the corrected map enables further movement. It realizes this paradigm with revisable occupancy and with exploration from reachable viewpoints, which continues the search when no frontier remains, and verifies the target across views before stopping. On three benchmarks, ERA achieves state-of-the-art performance among training-free methods without depth sensors. Its map and exploration components also raise VLFM's success from 10.8% to 61.7% under the same predicted depth. We further deploy ERA on a real robot.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-view visual augmentation often seeks consistency across cameras, but it is unclear which forms of consistency matter for downstream control. We use simulation-state replay to manipulate cross-view coupling while preserving the per-camera perturbation distributions, physical trajectories, and action labels. Across seven LIBERO manipulation tasks, marginal-matched changes in global appearance, ghost injection, and target color have small average costs for a late-fusion Diffusion Policy: point estimates are near zero or negative, and the largest upper endpoint of the 90% posterior credible intervals is 2.55 percentage points. In contrast, geometrically anti-coupled observations reduce success by 3.30 percentage points relative to coupled observations (90% CrI [0.64, 6.28]), with displacement magnitude, direction marginals, and the number of affected views matched. Camera interventions reveal substantial dependence on wrist-view geometry, while feature analysis shows appearance sensitivity patterns associated with the factors randomized during training. ACT shows a higher target-color cost estimate than DP, with uncertainty that includes zero and sensitivity to task composition. Within the tested tasks and policy configurations, these findings motivate selective consistency constraints: appearance coupling can sometimes be relaxed, whereas task-relevant geometry and cross-view spatial correspondence warrant protection.

</details>


### [208] [Beyond Prediction Gain: LASR as a Metric for Action-Grounded Latents in World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17775)
*Yu Zhou,Changsong Lei,Yixiang Wei,Kairui Wen,Huankun Sheng,Ming Li,Yeying Fan,Yu-Hui Wen,Yong-Jin Liu*

Abstract: World models that support planning, control, and interpretable reasoning require latent representations that genuinely encode action. Yet the large-scale video data from which such models are learned overwhelmingly lack action annotations. Latent-action models infer action-like variables from observation sequences, enabling action-aware learning from unlabeled videos or trajectories. A fundamental concern, however, remains: a latent that improves future prediction may simply encode the current state, episode progress, task identity, or other predictive shortcuts. Existing evaluation practices---reconstruction loss, rollout fidelity, action recovery, or downstream performance---cannot isolate this distinction. We introduce the Latent Action Support Ratio (LASR), a dimensionless metric that measures, under offline evaluation on held-out data, whether a learned latent provides predictive support for action-related targets beyond the current state and non-action alternatives. LASR provides two complementary summaries: GeoLASR captures typical support, while WorstLASR identifies the weakest case. We instantiate the LASR benchmark with recorded actions and corresponding physical state changes as targets, episode-held-out splits, one predictor type for all comparisons, and positive and negative controls. Experiments on recorded robot manipulation data and a matched multi-task panel demonstrate that LASR detects known action information, rejects representative non-action shortcuts, and enables graded comparisons among imperfect latent representations. We further use LASR to train and audit a lightweight residual adapter, obtaining descriptively better point estimates whose uncertainty is assessed with paired comparisons. LASR thus provides a principled offline protocol for measuring action-related predictive support in world-model latents.

</details>

Abstract: Service robots in domestic and office environments must identify users to provide personalized services based on their preferences and previous interactions. This is challenging because only a few observations may be available for learning, while observation conditions change owing to appearance, environmental noise, and utterance content. To address this challenge, this study proposes a multimodal person concept model that learns person concepts from a small number of observations, without person labels, and enables robust identification under changing conditions. The model employs a nonparametric Bayesian generative model based on the Chinese restaurant process and integrates visual, acoustic, and linguistic features extracted using Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP) and Contrastive Language-Audio Pretraining (CLAP), followed by principal component analysis (PCA). We evaluated six participants under eight conditions in which appearance changes, additive noise, and transcription errors were introduced individually and in combination. Averaged over the eight conditions, the proposed model achieved an identification accuracy of 0.89 while inferring the number of person concepts from the observations, which is comparable to k-means clustering that was supplied with the true number of participants (0.88). We also examined PCA dimensionality, modality availability, and variation strength, and demonstrated the model on a Toyota Human Support Robot. These results show that multimodal person concept learning supports robust person identification under changing conditions, and that identification can succeed even when the number of concepts is not correctly recovered. The project page and demonstration video are available at https://okitamisan01.github.io/person_concept/.

</details>

Abstract: Safe navigation of robot collectives requires coordinating motion and deformation so that the collective can traverse constrained environments while ensuring each robot's safety. Existing controllers for swarmalator collectives largely rely on local control, where each robot receives an individual command, which causes the control and communication requirements to grow with the number of agents. We introduce a broadcast control formulation in which a single spatially affine field governs both the motion and deformation of the collective. The scalar component of the field regulates collective size, and its spatial gradient generates collective translation through non-reciprocal interactions. Both components enter the collective dynamics directly, which allows per-robot wall, actuation, and cohesion constraints to be enforced using control barrier functions within a single quadratic program for the whole collective. The resulting controller continuously adapts the collective size to the local environment, and enables safe passage through corridors narrower than the unconstrained collective without pre-planned resizing. We further derive how the available actuation budget determines the reachable speed and minimum passable corridor width, and we demonstrate that two independently driven collectives can simultaneously traverse a shared junction using distinct fixed phase labels without inter-collective coordination.

</details>

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has enabled robust humanoid locomotion, yet training recipes (rewards, architectures, hyperparameters) remain hard to reuse across robots with different native joint coordinates, since policies output native joint angles while action scales, bounds, gains, and penalties are defined in those robot-specific coordinates. Existing transmission models convert native targets into actuator commands but do not standardize the coordinate interface exposed to the policy. We introduce a body-aligned virtual joint space upstream of this mapping: policy actions and observations follow a common anatomical convention, and a deterministic kinematic layer maps virtual targets to native ones, leaving the existing transmission and low-level control stack unchanged. Under a single unmodified recipe on three humanoids with different leg-axis layouts, it is the only interface that tracks accurately, keeps the feet aligned with the travel direction, and trains stably on all three, and on Unitree G1 hardware it reduces heading drift by nearly a factor of six.

</details>

Abstract: Large language and vision-language models have substantially advanced robotic behavior planning from natural-language instructions and visual observations. However, safe manipulation in tabletop environments remains challenging because the safety constraints required for a task depend on the surrounding scene and may not be explicitly specified in the instruction. We propose a safety-aware behavior planning framework based on an explicit Constraint Space that stores reusable textual safety rules. Rather than retrieving rules solely by semantic similarity, we formulate constraint selection as task- and environment-conditioned applicability learning. A lightweight query-conditioned dual-tower model identifies the constraints applicable to the current context, which are then provided together with the original task and image to a multimodal behavior planner. To handle incorrect constraint selections and previously unseen hazards, we introduce a multimodal Safety Inspector that evaluates generated primitive plans, reconstructs the complete applicable constraint set, and provides corrective planning feedback. Validated inspection feedback is further used for test-time adaptation of constraint applicability and parameter-efficient adaptation of the Safety Inspector through LoRA, while newly identified constraints are incorporated into the Constraint Space. The resulting framework integrates explicit safety knowledge representation,context-aware constraint selection, corrective safety inspection, and test-time knowledge adaptation for safety-aware robotic behavior planning.

</details>

Abstract: Reaching a goal state in a complex environment often requires a robot to overcome challenging terrain and obstacles. Which routes are accessible depends on the robot's physical design, the control policy it uses, and how it modifies its surroundings. We present a kinodynamic rapidly-exploring random tree (RRT) planner that uses physics simulation to exploit skills encoded in the control policy, which allows it to find a path in the scene with respect to the robot's capabilities. The planner samples states from a reduced state space in 3D around the scene and expands the RRT toward each sample using policy rollouts. We guide sampling with a preplanned path for a simple mass-point model to focus exploration on promising regions leading toward the goal state, reducing planning time by up to 63%. Simulation experiments with quadrupeds, humanoids, and wheeled robots demonstrate that our approach supports drop-in integration of various robots and control policies, exploiting their physical and policy capabilities without modifying the underlying planning algorithm. Predefined environmental modifications, such as placing a plank across a gap to build a bridge, will automatically be picked up by the planner as the robot's reachable space expands.

</details>
