Abstract: Semantic exploration methods for object-goal navigation use large language models (LLMs) to score how strongly a target object is associated with a room type or with a nearby object. This paper considers obtaining from the LLM a spatial prior instead: a distribution over the position of the target in a room. We evaluate whether two open-weight LLMs, Qwen3.5-27B and Gemma-4-31B-it, can provide such a prior. Using real room layouts from CubiCasa5K and ScanNet, we measure the likelihood the models assign to real and perturbed layouts, and the layouts they generate. The object frequencies and room sizes of the generated rooms follow the room type, but the proximity of the objects to the walls does not follow the differences between room types found in real rooms. The likelihood distinguishes real layouts from perturbed ones partly through their geometry and partly through the agreement between the coordinates and a field that states the wall each object touches; for the movable furniture of ScanNet, it is higher for layouts with all objects piled near the room center than for the real layouts in most rooms. When an object is given in the prompt, the models place a related object relative to it. When the wall of each object is given, they place most objects against that wall, but do not keep all objects inside a given room rectangle. As a prior for object search, the generated placements reduce the searched area relative to a uniform prior, but by less than a third of the reduction obtained with a prior built from real layouts, and by less than two thirds when both priors are spatially smoothed. These results support the use of LLMs for object-room associations and for writing coordinates under constraints determined by another component, followed by a geometric check, but not as a source of placement priors where real layouts are available.

</details>

Abstract: Independently pretrained language models and vision-language-action (VLA) policies provide complementary building blocks for embodied agents, but connecting them does not automatically produce coordinated behavior. The VLA may generate actions that diverge from the LLM's intended subtask and, upon regaining control after a correction, undo the promising state restored by the LLM. We introduce ESI (Embodied Self-Improvement), an automated flywheel that uses environmental interaction to align reasoning and action. ESI treats the modular agent as the unit of post-training, repeatedly refining the prompts that guide the LLM, the shared skill interface that expresses its intent, and the VLA parameters that realize that intent, then redeploying these components together. Our key insight is that collaboration itself can supply supervision for improving this interface. The LLM's subtask decisions provide semantic supervision for what the policy should execute, while its effective corrections and subsequent student continuations provide action-level supervision for recovery and handoff. The student also learns from its own effective execution, allowing locally useful experience to contribute even when the overall episode fails. On ten selected RoboDojo tasks, ESI achieves a mean Score of 62.03 and a mean success rate of 49.41% using an LLM-VLA agent without generating task-specific control code. On both metrics, it outperforms the published reference results of all hybrid embodied agents, including the Agent+WAM system GO-3 Nano Preview (47.27 Score and 36.60% success), as well as all compared code-based methods.

</details>

Abstract: On small single-board computers (SBCs), GNSS signal acquisition is a major CPU load. This paper proposes using the neural processing unit (NPU) of an SBC not for machine learning but as an accelerator for the parallel code search in GNSS signal acquisition. The discrete Fourier transform (DFT) used in acquisition is decomposed by the four-step DFT into matrix products of small DFTs and expressed by $1\times1$ convolutions, element-wise products, and transposes, which NPUs execute efficiently. Furthermore, we propose a method that divides the DFT, the non-coherent integration, and the peak detection between the CPU and the NPU according to the configuration of the NPU (an NPU with its own memory connected via PCIe, or an NPU sharing memory with the CPU). The proposed method is implemented in the open-source software GNSS receiver Pocket SDR and evaluated on a Raspberry Pi 5 combined with the external AX8850 NPU and on the NPU built into the SoC (RK3588) of the ROCK 5C, a small SBC of comparable size. Compared with acquisition by an optimized CPU implementation, the acquisition of short codes on the Raspberry Pi 5 with the AX8850 is 3.0-3.8 times faster. In the evaluation of the whole receiver, the number of tracking channels that reach their first lock within 30 s with the NPU is 1.5 times that with the CPU, and the time until 90% of the channels are locked is reduced from 66 s to 26 s. Furthermore, because the CPU load of acquisition is reduced, the number of tracking channels that can be processed in real time increases from about 175 to about 230. These results show that using the NPU on SBCs with limited CPU resources is effective for achieving both GNSS signal acquisition and tracking. The implementation is publicly available as open source under the name PocketSDR-NPU.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have shown strong semantic and spatial reasoning capabilities in 3D environments. Recently, connecting VLMs with model-predictive controllers (MPC) has emerged as a paradigm for combining strong semantic reasoning with physics-based planning. However, connecting these two for contact-rich robotic manipulation requires a task representation that bridges the two and captures how physical interactions can be reasoned by VLMs. In this work, we introduce Objectives as Policies (OaP), a framework that connects a pretrained VLM to a shared sampling-based MPC through an editable objective program. Each program specifies a sequence of stages for completing a task, combining running and terminal costs from a cost library with stage completion conditions. Given a natural-language task instruction and scene observation, we build a simulation-based digital twin through real-to-sim, and the VLM generates an objective program that is executed by the MPC in the simulation. The program is iteratively updated by the VLM based on MPC rollouts, before being deployed in the real world. We evaluate our framework in both simulation and the real world on four tabletop manipulation tasks, and show that it outperforms strong baselines. Additional videos and examples are available on the project website: https://zihaolu001.github.io/oap-project/

</details>

Abstract: Vision-based perceptive locomotion has enabled legged robots to achieve agile and terrain-aware locomotion across complex environments. However, the reliability of such systems heavily depends on visual observations, which are inherently susceptible to occlusion, reflective surfaces, and illumination variations, often resulting in catastrophic locomotion failures under degraded perception conditions. In this paper, we present PROOF (Perceptive Robustness via Out-of-Distribution Fallback for Legged Locomotion), a perceptive locomotion framework that enables robust locomotion under severe visual disturbances. Our method adopts a two-stage teacher-student learning paradigm that explicitly models perception reliability: the teacher learns both perception-driven and proprioceptive fallback behaviors, while the student predicts exteroceptive features with uncertainty and suppresses them under out-of-distribution inputs. We evaluate our method on a Unitree Go2 quadruped equipped with a RealSense D435 depth camera in real-world settings. Under normal visual conditions, our method enables the robot to successfully perform high-dynamic parkour maneuvers, including traversing large gaps, elevated platforms, and narrow beam terrains. Under severe visual degradation, such as heavy occlusion and strong illumination changes, the robot continues to maintain stable and robust locomotion performance.

</details>


### [219] [Envisioning Agentic Robotics: A New Era of Robotic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18359)
*Grid Robotics,:,Qingfeng Lan,Duo Li,Siyao Li,Yiheng Lyu,Shijian Lu,Zhenghao Qi,Shizun Wang,Xinchao Wang,Ming Yang,Zhiqin Yang,Zuhao Yang,Tao Yu*

Abstract: The release of GPT-6 Astra marks the emergence of a new paradigm in robotics: agentic robotics. Within weeks, frontier models with agentic harnesses matched or exceeded task-specific policies on many embodied benchmarks. Instead of training a dedicated policy for each family of tasks, an agentic robotic system applies a foundation model to open-world embodied tasks. It operates in a closed loop: observing the environment, interpreting user intent, reasoning about the task, invoking skills and tools, verifying outcomes, and adapting its strategy. Despite these advances, the community has yet to converge on a shared definition, a reliable evaluation framework, or a research roadmap. We argue that Embodied System 2, realized by a foundation model together with an embodied harness, forms the decision-making core of agentic robotics. Its central challenge is to translate general-purpose reasoning into effective and verifiable actions under physical constraints such as limited reaction time, mechanical limits, unpredictable environment changes, and irreversible consequences. Motivated by this perspective, we propose an evaluation framework that measures both model capabilities and closed-loop system performance. Furthermore, we identify the principles that carry over from digital to physical agents and the challenges that must be addressed to achieve reliable physical autonomy. On top of the Embodied System 2 and the evaluation framework, we envision a research roadmap toward sustained autonomy: the ability to perform work safely and reliably with progressively broader responsibility, from executing individual primitives to fulfilling standing roles.

</details>

Abstract: Automation of heavy machinery offers the opportunity to improve productivity in mechanized industries such as construction, mining, and material handling. Yet, automation of such machines is challenged by their complex dynamics, in part because of hydraulic actuation that involves fluids, flexible elements, and nonlinear delays. A concomitant challenge is the size and weight of these machines, which can make experiments on the physical systems exceedingly dangerous. Here, we show that \ac{RL} in simulation, followed by transfer of the learned policies from simulation to reality, can successfully automate acrobatic maneuvers that push a heavy construction machine to the limits of its actuation and have only previously been accomplished by a small number of elite human operators in official world-record performances. We trained policies that perform autonomous acrobatic maneuvers on a John Deere 332G, an operational 5-tonne skid-steer loader. The policies can autonomously swing the machine up onto two wheels and, while balancing on two wheels, drive over rough terrain, trace out specified trajectories, spin around, and swing the bucket back and forth before landing it safely back onto four wheels. This work demonstrates that \ac{RL} and sim-to-real transfer enable dynamic autonomous control of heavy machinery, directly contributing to greater automation in heavy equipment sectors. Videos: https://sites.google.com/leggedrobotics.com/heavy-metal-acrobatics

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models adapt web-pretrained vision-language models to output robot actions, so that web knowledge carries over to physical control. In under 4 years the field has moved from RT-2, the first VLA, to open models that fine-tune on a single consumer GPU and to proprietary generalists that clean unseen homes. This survey reviews 88 works from December 2022 to October 2026, mainly on manipulation and navigation, plus humanoid control, world-action models and evaluation. We follow a VLA through its life cycle, from model design to training and deployment, along seven axes. Model design covers (a) inputs and memory, what the model observes; (b) architecture, how its pipeline is built; (c) action output, how it produces motor commands; and (d) intermediate predictions, what else it predicts, from reasoning steps to future observations. Training covers (e) pretraining data, the mix of robot, web, human and simulated data, and (f) post-training, adaptation to the target robot. Deployment covers (g) real-time execution, keeping pace with the robot. We analyse 22 models on these axes and report their training data, model size, inference speed and hardware. Three trends emerge: (i) training data has broadened, from about 10,000 hours of teleoperated robot data ($π_0$) to 23,000 community-collected episodes (SmolVLA) and 20,000 hours of egocentric human video (GR00T N1.7); (ii) model sizes have split: most recent models have 3-7 billion parameters, while a new group under one billion runs on consumer GPUs; and (iii) generalist VLAs are emerging that handle both manipulation and navigation, such as UniVLA and Qwen-VLA. We also compare VLAs with world-action models, which predict future observations as well as actions. We review evidence that some VLAs with near-perfect benchmark scores fail when objects or the camera move slightly, and open up further research questions.

</details>


### [222] [The State Is Not the Bottleneck: A Paged-State Gated-DeltaNet Tile on a Tri-Mode Multicast Mesh, Bit-Exact from RTL to Silicon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18422)

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Linear-attention language models replace a growing key-value cache with a recurrent state of fixed size. On an edge FPGA, this state can exceed the on-chip memory and must be paged to external memory. This paper asks whether such paging dominates the cost of decoding (generating tokens). We built a hardware tile that computes one complete layer of a custom 369-million-parameter hybrid Gated DeltaNet model on an Agilex 5 FPGA and pages its recurrent state. The model state needs 6.2 MiB, whereas the device has 0.87 MiB of block memory. Paging adds 26.0 MB of memory traffic per decoded token, at most 11.2 percent of the calculated total. On the board at 75 MHz, with synthetic weights, one token takes 11.15 ms in this layer. The matrix-vector products with the weights take about 62 percent of this time and the state phase, including the paging, 13 percent. The tile is one node of a multicast mesh of tri-mode processing elements. We measure the error of the fixed-point arithmetic against floating point and check that the hardware agrees bit for bit with a fixed-point reference. In simulation, the state logic agrees with the reference on all 18 recurrent layers. On the board, six builds pass all 30 runs of 64 tokens and two runs of 1,024 tokens. For this model and memory interface, the delivery of the weights limits the decoding performance, and the recurrent state does not. State paging is therefore practical for such a model on an edge FPGA.

</details>

Abstract: AI-enabled remote patient monitoring is evolving beyond passive data collection toward agentic AI systems that interpret patient states, coordinate care, and initiate action across domestic and clinical settings. This structured scoping review finds that mature agentic care has not yet emerged: Most systems remain human led, while stronger decision-action coupling and embodied AI remain largely confined to prototypes and pilots. Healthcare robotics makes the transition from monitoring to action especially visible by translating AI-generated recommendations and decisions into speech, movement, prompting, physical assistance, escalation, and care coordination, making embodiment, clinical integration, human oversight, and governance fundamental design requirements. Further progress requires safe and interoperable care architectures in which intelligent components operate through clinically validated and accountable workflows. We propose agentic robotics as a future direction for supervised systems embedded in the care ecosystem, connecting AI agents and robots with clinicians, caregivers, and patients through auditable workflows that can be interrupted and remain subject to human oversight and explicit AI governance.

</details>

Abstract: Cooperative self-play can teach a humanoid to receive an object without any human data, but the partner it meets after training is never the one it trained with; we show that a fluent self-play handover policy breaks with unfamiliar partners, identify the main cause, and repair it. In a physics-based parcel handover between a Unitree G1 and a learned giver, both trained by alternating PPO self-play, the receiver completes 95% and 90% of handovers with its own partner (two training seeds) but only 31% with scripted partners it has never met. Paired interventions isolate the cause: under a shared reward the learned giver always starts its offer at once, so the receiver learns a single rendezvous, and delaying its own partner by one second cuts success from 95% to 6% (87% to 34% in the second seed). We introduce hidden start-delay randomisation (HSD), a one-line rule on the learned partner that the receiver never observes. After 300 further updates, success with unseen scripted partners rises from 31% to 80% in both seeds, against 35--41% for a budget-matched control. The gain holds on a fresh scenario seed, on eleven held-out partner conditions and on partners that start later than any training delay; it cuts high-force contact three- to five-fold and needs no retraining of the partner.

</details>

Abstract: Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) accelerate scientific research by integrating robotics, automated experimentation, and machine learning to execute experiments autonomously. Biology and chemistry have adopted these systems. However, increasing SDL complexity heightens susceptibility to unexpected faults in automated workflows, highlighting the need for efficient recovery mechanisms. In this study, we investigate telerobotic interventions to enhance SDL resilience against unexpected failures. We developed a teleoperation interface enabling human operators to resolve disruptions during automated tasks. The interface enables precise control of a six-degree-of-freedom (DOF) robotic arm (UR3e) using a 3D mouse (3Dconnexion SpaceMouse Pro). Two velocity-based controller strategies were evaluated: (i) a coupled mode mapping all six DOFs to the 3D mouse's central knob and (ii) a decoupled mode separating translational and rotational motions. To evaluate the framework, teleoperators intervened across three SDL fault scenarios. In the first study, comparative trials showed that the decoupled controller was associated with shorter completion times and reduced rotational motion. In a second study, teleoperators performed an aiming task with and without reference-path guidance. Users were more target-driven and risk-tolerant without guidance but showed behavioral differences under different guidance strategies. These findings suggest that controller configuration and reference-path design influence operator performance during SDL fault recovery, informing teleoperation interface optimization. This work demonstrates the potential feasibility of teleoperation as a human-in-the-loop recovery mechanism for SDL workflows, combining robotic precision with human adaptability and problem-solving. These findings also provide a foundation for future studies in shared autonomy and semi-autonomous intervention.

</details>

Abstract: Harmful Algal Blooms (HABs) pose significant environmental, economic, and public-health challenges in coastal and inland water bodies, creating a need for scalable methods to collect spatially distributed water-quality data. This paper presents an autonomous surface vessel-based sensing system for in-situ water-quality monitoring related to harmful algal bloom detection. The system integrates a BlueBoat Autonomous Surface Vessel (ASV), a motorized winch for depth-controlled sonde deployment, a multiparameter water-quality sonde, side-scan sonar, and an event-camera payload. Autonomous waypoint missions were used to collect measurements across selected sampling regions and multiple depths, enabling both horizontal and vertical characterization of environmental conditions. Field deployments were conducted in Lake Pontchartrain, Louisiana, with additional measurements collected in False River, Louisiana to increase dataset diversity. The collected measurements included algal pigment indicators, nutrient concentration, turbidity, temperature, and other water-quality parameters relevant to bloom formation. In addition, complementary sonar and event-camera data were collected during vehicle operation to provide contextual environmental information. A machine-learning classification approach was also evaluated using the collected water-quality measurements to support HAB and non-HAB classification. The results demonstrate the feasibility of using a modular ASV platform for repeatable, multi-modal water-quality data collection and support future development of autonomous monitoring systems.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous robots are widely used for persistent monitoring of spatiotemporal environments, where quantities of interest (QoI) change across space and time and require repeated measurements. In practice, robot motion can be affected by external disturbances that vary across the environment. When such external disturbances are not considered in trajectory optimization, it can lead to inaccurate predictions of robot motion and, consequently, the information acquired about the QoI during execution. However, estimating disturbances throughout the environment can be inefficient, as disturbances in regions with little overlap with monitoring trajectories have limited impact on monitoring performance. We therefore propose a two-stage informative planning framework for unknown, spatially varying but time-invariant disturbances, combining informed disturbance sampling with disturbance-aware trajectory optimization. First, we identify where QoI measurements are needed and generate alternative paths to these locations using the environment geometry. We then prioritize disturbance measurements based on their expected reduction of disturbance uncertainty along these paths. Second, rather than optimizing information acquisition based on nominal robot motion, we incorporate the learned disturbance belief into trajectory optimization to account for its effects on robot motion and information acquisition. Across four simulation scenarios, the proposed method reduces disturbance-learning time by 25.0--52.3 percent compared with environment-wide estimation and identifies paths that appear costly under nominal dynamics but become preferable when disturbances are considered.

</details>

Abstract: Accurate friction modeling in continuum robots is essential for precise motion control and path planning. However, current approaches make predictions only for the mechanism's internal friction that is limited by the boundary forces of the Coulomb friction cone, which fail to characterize the static friction and motion hysteresis. In this paper, we propose a novel friction model based on complementarity constraints under the Coulomb friction assumption. This model predicts various friction effects caused by environmental interactions, including both static and sliding frictions and hysteresis behavior, as well as the corresponding robot deformation induced by these interactions, which are crucial for the practical motion planning and control of continuum robots. Additionally, real-time implementation of this model can be achieved, meeting the demand for computational efficiency in robotic applications. Experimental validations demonstrated that this model is able to reduce the maximum tip position error from 45.4 mm in the frictionless model to 5.85 mm in our friction-included model with a 287.9 mm-long tendon-driven continuum robot.

</details>

Abstract: Robot-use agents (RUAs) have emerged as a promising approach to general-purpose robot control. However, strong performance has so far been achieved only by frontier proprietary models such as GPT-6 Astra and Claude Fable 5.2, whereas open-weight models remain substantially less capable. We show that an open-weight 27B reasoning model can be trained to use real robots natively through reinforcement learning, entirely in simulation and without demonstrations. Like a computer-use agent (CUA), the model observes the environment, reasons in a chain of thought, and generates the next end-effector pose, without any harness. We build a Post-training Engine for Agentic Robotics (PEAR) and use it to train the model on simulated robot tasks at scale with robot-use reinforcement learning (RURL), mirroring how frontier models acquire computer use. We find that the skills acquired in simulation generalize well beyond the training tasks: they transfer to an unseen robot, to the real world, and to novel instructions. After training on LIBERO and RoboDojo in simulation, the model succeeds in 60% of our trials on a real dual-arm robot, whereas the base model succeeds in none. On LIBERO, training raises the success rate from 1.6% to 74.4%, approaching GPT-6 Astra and GPT-6.1 Sol at maximum reasoning effort. Compared with $π_{0.5}$, a vision-language-action model fine-tuned on demonstrations, our model is substantially more robust to changes in the task itself. On LIBERO-Pro, $π_{0.5}$ succeeds in only 0.75% of episodes when assigned a new task in a familiar scene whereas our model succeeds in 57.4%. Finally, the model retains its general capabilities, with no measurable degradation on nine reasoning, coding and vision benchmarks. These results highlight robot-use reinforcement learning as a promising component of foundation-model post-training.

</details>

Abstract: This paper addresses the tracking control of block strict-feedback nonlinear systems subject to time-varying asymmetric actuator and full state constraints. To that end, we develop a time-varying Admissibility-Preserving Control (APC) framework, in which the Admissibility-Preserving Input Realization (APIR) generates the plant input through a dynamic realization. The APIR guarantees input admissibility under the moving actuator set whenever each actuator bound contracts slower than the decay rate of the realization, and a closed-form lower bound on its gain is derived. The state constraints are handled by a logarithmic barrier coordinate at every backstepping step, with widths measured from the virtual control to the moving envelope of the corresponding state. We prove that every state lies inside its envelope, with each virtual control only required to remain inside the envelope of the next state, while the tracking error converges to zero exponentially. Simulations demonstrate the efficacy of the proposed safety-critical control scheme.

</details>

Abstract: Reachability analysis is a popular technique for verifying the safety of a dynamical system under uncertainty, where the uncertainty is often represented by zonotopes. As the size of the zonotope representation typically grows as the reachability analysis progresses, it must be repeatedly reduced at the cost of additional conservatism. While various methods exist to reduce the size of the representation, combined advantages are not fully leveraged as only a single reduction method is typically used throughout the analysis. In this work, we show how dynamically choosing which method to use can yield significantly tighter results, thereby easing verification. This is realized by introducing a heuristic, derived from model predictive control, that accounts for upcoming propagation of the current zonotope. We argue that order reduction is not merely a post-processing step after propagation but also a pre-processing step that shapes how the zonotope evolves under the system dynamics. We evaluate our approach, RAZeR, on nonlinear benchmarks taken from the ARCH-COMP, where we demonstrate that RAZeR produces tighter zonotopes at a negligible performance overhead.

</details>

Abstract: We propose a method to learn a game-theoretic model predictive control (GT-MPC) law by imitation from input and output data of a running multi-agent system. By interpreting the recorded inputs as the first moves of the equilibria of a game played by the agents, we learn the GT-MPC weights under hard input constraints and soft output constraints, without requiring the agents to reveal their objectives. We also impose that the resulting generalized Nash equilibrium (GNE) problem is strongly monotone, which ensures the existence and uniqueness of the equilibrium and, hence, that the learned GT-MPC law is well defined and can be evaluated efficiently online by variational GNE-seeking algorithms for linear-quadratic games. Moreover, strong monotonicity implies that the equilibrium is almost everywhere differentiable with respect to the references and the weights, which enables automatic differentiation for gradient-based optimization. In addition, we promote closed-loop stability of the unconstrained equilibrium feedback by incorporating a penalty that involves a jointly learned quadratic Lyapunov function. Learning a GT-MPC formulation enables us to explain the agents' objectives, reproduce their behavior in simulation in new scenarios, optimize their set-points, design mechanisms, and enforce additional constraints. A numerical example illustrates the approach.

</details>

Abstract: Data centers are now one of the fastest-growing loads on the bulk power system, and most of their demand reaches the grid through power converters inside uninterruptible power supplies (UPS). Whether these loads can take part in sub-synchronous control interaction (SSCI), as wind and HVDC converters do, has not been studied extensively. We examine this question with impedance-based frequency scans at the point of common coupling (PCC). Using a detailed proprietary model (INS-ISU), we scan the motor, variable-frequency-drive (VFD), lighting, and UPS-fed information-technology (IT) branches of a representative facility separately. Only the UPS in double-conversion mode shows negative resistance; it covers the whole sub-synchronous band and continues up to about 170 Hz. The other branches, and the UPS in bypass mode, stay passive. We then repeat the scans on three public models from PNNL, TAMU, and NLR. None of them shows negative resistance in bypass mode, while three of the four models do in double-conversion mode. Lowering the load from rated to light load makes the negative resistance deeper, as expected for a constant-power load, so a lightly loaded facility in double-conversion mode is the worst case. Raising the UPS DC-link capacitance pushes the negative resistance into a narrow band around the fundamental. These findings give a basis for screening data centers for SSCI during interconnection studies.

</details>

Abstract: This paper presents a constraint-aware reinforcement learning (CARL) controller for battery energy storage system (BESS) sizing and photovoltaic (PV) ramp-rate smoothing. Rapid PV power fluctuations caused by cloud transitions can introduce operational and stability challenges in power systems. To mitigate these impacts, grid codes impose ramp-rate limits (RRL) on PV power injected to the grid. BESSs can support such grid codes by absorbing or supplying power during rapid variations. However, the extent to which BESSs can reduce PV-induced variability depends on (i) the BESS power and energy capacities; and (ii) the adopted controller. Thus, BESS sizing and controller design are coupled. Conventional moving-average control can increase battery usage due to filter-induced lag, while ramp-rate control is reactive and does not account for the long-term impact of actions on battery state of charge and future ramp events. Optimization-based methods can reduce BESS requirements by anticipating future PV variations, but their performance depends on accurate PV forecasts. To address these limitations, the CARL controller learns a causal state-feedback policy from historical PV data without requiring forecasts during deployment. Ramp-rate, converter-power, and state-of-charge constraints are incorporated into a causal feasibility interval that guarantees feasible actions by definition. A full-horizon input-convex neural network (ICNN)-based optimization is developed as a noncausal benchmark for the minimum achievable BESS requirements. Evaluation using three real-world PV datasets shows that, at a 10\%/min ramp-rate limit, CARL reduces peak BESS power by 8.7--10.4\% and energy capacity by 47.5--62.6\% relative to conventional ramp-rate control while maintaining the grid-codes compliance.

</details>

Abstract: Damping control of a forced oscillation may reduce amplitudes at certain locations while increasing them elsewhere. This paper introduces Pareto improvement as a criterion for assessing such control: at least one nodal oscillation amplitude decreases, while none increases across all other nodes. The analysis considers active-power forcing sources and active-power control injections in connected swing networks with positive inertia and damping. Necessary conditions for Pareto improvement feasibility are derived from the normalized nodal responses. It is proven that for every network with a single oscillation source and a single actuator, at least one positive forcing frequency is shown to preclude Pareto improvement, regardless of the amplitude and phase of the control input. A three-node counterexample shows that this limitation does not persist with multiple actuators. PSS/E simulations of a 6-bus benchmark system and a modified IEEE 39-bus system validated the analysis. These findings suggest the value of assessing control effects across the network and further investigating multi-actuator damping control.

</details>

Abstract: The growing role of space computing in time-critical satellite services makes the long-term reliability of onboard artificial intelligence (AI) a mission-level requirement. Model drift can undermine this reliability through critical-observation suppression or noncritical priority flooding, displacing both mission information and evidence for model correction. To address this drift-induced evidence displacement, this article presents Orbit-AWARE, a safeguard-first lifecycle management (LCM) architecture. A comparison of existing approaches motivates cooperative Data, Cognitive, and Trust Planes for continuous monitoring, event-triggered agentic orchestration, and deterministic governance. The architecture retains a model-independent sample stream before degradation is confirmed and coordinates evidence selection and exchange for diagnosis and ground-assisted adaptation. Candidate updates are verified before control over data disposition is restored. In an xBD-based emergency Earth observation (EO) case study, Orbit-AWARE delivers 14.3\% more critical observations and achieves 8.3\% higher sustainable mission utility than the Rule-Based Drift Loop under a common 10\% downlink budget. Finally, we discuss future research directions for extending LCM through task-aware reliability assessment, memory- and tool-enabled onboard agents, and evidence-centric space networking. This work makes evidence availability and recovery authorization explicit design objectives for dependable onboard AI services.

</details>

Abstract: The increasing penetration of renewable energy and the rapid growth of AI data centers are introducing significant challenges to power system frequency security. There is an urgent need to identify grid-compatible resources capable of providing fast frequency support. This paper proposes a frequency-security-aware real-time economic dispatch (RTED) model that enables data center (DC) uninterruptible power supply (UPS) systems to provide steady-state power balancing, inertia response, and primary frequency response (PFR) services. Specifically, tractable analytical frequency-security constraints are derived and incorporated into the RTED framework through second-order cone programming (SOCP) formulation. The proposed model jointly optimizes energy, reserve, and inertia to minimize system operation cost while preventing under-frequency load shedding (UFLS). Numerical validation on a modified IEEE 39-bus system demonstrates that the proposed RTED model with UPS flexibility can economically support system operation and significantly improve frequency security under active-power deficit disturbances.

Abstract: As energy storage assumes a larger role in electricity systems, market operation must coordinate its use across time to support reliability during periods of system stress. Minimum state-of-charge (SOC) targets provide a means of preserving energy for these periods, but their design shapes reliability, operating efficiency, and storage compensation. This paper evaluates alternative SOC target designs in a California-based day-ahead and real-time electricity market model. The comparison examines targets based on day-ahead schedules or rolling centralized dispatch, enforced at the individual-resource or system level, across seasons, stress conditions, and participant information settings. The results show that retaining more energy does not necessarily reduce load shedding: targets that restrict discharge can limit storage support during shortages. Under critical summer conditions, individual targets derived from rolling centralized dispatch reduce weekly load shedding by approximately 83--84\% relative to operation without targets. Lower RT clearing costs and average prices can coexist with higher storage compensation, reflecting the role of day-ahead positions and real-time deviations in determining settlement outcomes. These findings highlight the importance of coordinating energy retention with timely discharge and network deliverability when designing SOC requirements for storage participation in electricity markets.

</details>

Abstract: Accurate prediction of frequency dynamics under varying network topologies is essential for rapid frequency security assessment, whereas repeated electromagnetic transient (EMT) simulations are computationally intensive. This paper develops a topology-aware graph neural network with a radial basis function Kolmogorov-Arnold network (RBF-KAN) regression head, termed RK-GNN, to predict frequency nadir and maximum absolute rate of change of frequency (RoCoF). Scenarios combine load scaling, regional load redistribution, and generator redispatch with intact, N-1, and N-2 transmission topologies. After checks for network connectivity, generator reference limits, and numerical consistency, the retained scenarios are simulated in PSCAD to construct the reference dataset. The RK-GNN is evaluated against a conventional deep neural network (DNN) and a baseline GNN. On the IEEE 39-bus system, the RK-GNN reduces the mean absolute errors (MAEs) of nadir and RoCoF by 48% and 59% relative to the DNN and by 42% and 61% relative to the baseline GNN. Incorporating the bridge indicator and disturbance hop distance yields further MAE reductions of 27% and 54%. These results show that the proposed topology-aware architecture provides accurate prediction of both frequency metrics under varying topologies and substantially reduces the need for repeated EMT simulations.

Abstract: Fast and accurate simulation of electric motors is essential for their design, fault diagnosis and control. Magnetic equivalent circuits (MECs) offer a computationally efficient alternative to the finite element method (FEM), yet their flux tube representation poorly captures the air-gap field. This creates a challenge, as the air gap stores most of the magnetic energy and governs the electromagnetic torque and, in rotor-eccentric motors, the unbalanced magnetic pull (UMP). Existing air-gap permeance expressions vary widely in their results for torque ripple and UMP and account for tooth-normal eccentricity only. This paper proposes the dual-term air-gap permeance (DTAP) model, a closed-form expression for the air-gap permeance between a stator and a rotor tooth that accounts for tooth-normal and tooth-tangential eccentricity, with and without rotor skew. Its parameters are identified from a FEM model of the air gap and the complete set of tooth heads, which includes the shielding by the neighbouring teeth. For three motors, DTAP reproduces the FEM air-gap permeance with a 2.8-4.8 times smaller error than the state of the art, at a comparable computational cost. Implemented in the MEC of an eccentric induction motor, it reproduces the torque ripple within 1.6% and the peak UMP within 1.3% of the reference, i.e., with 2.8 and 27 times smaller deviations than the state of the art. DTAP thereby enables MEC to predict the torque pulsations and bearing loads of eccentric motors more accurately without sacrificing computational speed.

</details>

Abstract: A digital PID-like sliding-mode controller is proposed for a double integrator with a Lipschitz matched disturbance. A proper implicit discretization, matched to the exact sampled dynamics of the plant, attains the accuracy of the continuous-time controller without discretization chattering. Global finite-step convergence to a discrete-time sliding mode is established for two gain designs and every sampling time, using an implicit Lyapunov function and a computer-assisted certificate in exact arithmetic. The resulting controller provides third-order output accuracy, second-order accuracy for its derivative, and exact finite-time rejection of constant matched disturbances. These accuracy orders are proved to be optimal among deterministic causal controllers using the same sampled information.

Abstract: In grid-following converters, the synchronization path can transmit voltage disturbances to the current loop under weak or unbalanced grid conditions. This paper develops a Frenet-frame controller that derives its axes from the integral curve of the measured voltage at the point of common coupling. The tangential current governs active power, while the normal and binormal currents govern the transverse components of non-active power. Feedforward terms derived from the output filter model compensate curvature and torsion coupling, yielding identical nominal current dynamics across the axes. The controller operates without a phase-locked loop or sequence extraction. A reduced transverse model accounts for the finite bandwidth of geometric rate reconstruction and predicts a reduction in low-frequency coupling from voltage disturbances to current. Controller hardware-in-the-loop tests using real-time simulation of a three-phase four-wire converter show the shortest settling time after an active power reference step and the smallest peak power deviations during balanced voltage disturbances among the tested controllers. Under single-phase voltage unbalance, the method reduces peak binormal current and maximum phase-current total harmonic distortion relative to both benchmarks. It also settles to the commanded power in a weak-grid connection test where the phase-locked loop benchmark exhibits sustained oscillations.

</details>

Abstract: Strong damping feedback can substantially improve the transient synchronization stability of renewable energy converters employing virtual synchronous generator (VSG) control. However, conventional damping feedback uses the nominal frequency as its reference. When the frequency of a weak grid deviates from nominal, strong damping tends to keep the VSG frequency near nominal. This can sustain a frequency difference between the VSG and the grid and compromise transient synchronization. To address this problem, this paper proposes a complex-frequency-based method to estimate the grid reference frequency from local terminal measurements. The estimated frequency replaces the nominal reference in the VSG synchronization loop, so that damping acts on the frequency difference between the VSG and the real grid. Controller hardware-in-the-loop tests validate the effectiveness of the proposed method under grid frequency disturbances and its robustness to grid parameter uncertainty and adverse grid conditions.

</details>

Abstract: This paper addresses constrained output regulation for unknown linear systems by means of predictive control directly from input-output data. The objective is to reject disturbances and track references generated by a known exosystem, without identifying a plant model or solving the output regulator equations. The first step is a trajectory parametrization for plants driven by exosignals with known dynamics. % By exploiting the known exosystem eigenstructure, Willems et al's fundamental lemma is extended from autonomous input-output trajectories to trajectories coupled with exogenous modes. By exploiting the known exosystem eigenstructure, input-output trajectories driven by an exosignal of known phase are parametrized directly from data, which contains the affine version of Willems et al.'s fundamental lemma as a special case. This parametrization yields a nominal data-driven predictive control (DDPC) regulator, for which a Lyapunov argument proves exponential convergence to the regulator manifold. For periodic exosignals, the steady-state feedforward input is eliminated from the online problem through an incremental-input regularization, leading to an implementable regulator based on measured data and known exosystem information. Bounded output noise is handled by slack and coefficient regularization, with a regularization center computed offline from data; the resulting robust DDPC law is recursively feasible and practically stabilizing, with an ultimate regulation error that vanishes with the noise bound. A constrained lateral vehicle-control example with periodic road curvature illustrates disturbance rejection, input-constraint satisfaction, and the transition from exact regulation with noise-free data to practical regulation with noisy data.

</details>

Abstract: In networks of coupled oscillators, from neural circuits to power grids, damping is expected to suppress oscillations because of its dissipativity. Here we show that local damping can, counterintuitively, amplify oscillations along some input-output channels, while the network's global oscillation energy decreases and its spectral properties improve. The amplified oscillation arises from a latent resonance that lies in the spectrum yet barely manifests. The added passive damping reshapes this resonance by mixing it with adjacent eigenmodes. In random power-grid networks, damping can boost weakly responding channels by more than an order of magnitude, while subdominant channels can grow to exceed the originally strongest channel. Damping thus not only suppresses oscillations but also provides a way to redistribute oscillatory energy. For any passive network, the amplified response and its upper bound follow exactly from baseline quantities, offering a principled route for network design and control.

</details>

Abstract: This paper investigates distributed output-feedback stabilization of linear multichannel systems in which agents exchange state estimates without disclosing local control inputs or feedback gains. Remote control actions are treated as unknown inputs and geometrically decoupled from stable quotient-error dynamics. The proposed framework accommodates nontrivial intersections of local loss subspaces by retaining the coupling between the plant and common estimation errors. A Hurwitz condition on the coupled dynamics, together with a sufficient consensus-gain bound, guarantees exponential closed-loop convergence for every fixed feedback selection in a compact family. For polytopic feedback families, a quotient-preserving parameterization of output injections enables joint synthesis of nominal feedback and observer gains via alternating linear matrix inequalities, followed by a convex semidefinite program certifying a common consensus gain. A numerical example demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed design.

Abstract: Conventional scalar inertia characterizes only the low-frequency terminal behavior of grid-forming converters (GFMs) and cannot fully predict inertial energy delivery during short-duration frequency disturbances. This letter proposes a frequency-response-based method to assess finite-duration inertial support using complex terminal power--frequency responses, such as those provided by Network Frequency Perturbation (NFP) plots. For GFMs with nonzero steady-state droop, a duration-dependent energy-equivalent inertia is defined after subtracting the ideal static-droop contribution. An exact spectral relationship is established, expressing this metric as a duration-weighted integral of frequency-dependent inertia, without requiring knowledge of the internal controller structure and parameters. The duration dependence of the metric is further shown to encode observable oscillation frequencies and damping ratios. A coefficient-decoupling GFM control with identical scalar inertia and droop across different settings is used for verification.

</details>

Abstract: Integrated aircraft design combines models of aerodynamics, propulsion, mass, and mission performance in one design loop, so that the disciplines can be traded against each other from the earliest stage. For configurations with distributed electric propulsion (DEP), part of the control authority comes from the propulsion system itself, which directly affects the required size of the tail and should therefore be known while the aircraft is being sized. This paper presents a controllability measure that is evaluated for every design in the loop and returned to it as a design signal. The attainable moment set of each candidate design is computed from the surrugate models of the loop and it is compared with a required moment set. Since the low-fidelity surrugate models are uncertain, the comparison is formulated with probabilistic attainable moment sets: the uncertainty is propagated to the boundary of the set, which yields a margin for every direction in the moment space and the probability of violating the requirement. The design loop thereby informed whether a configuration provides sufficient control authority, on which axes it falls short, and how this changes with the design variables. The approach is demonstrated on a DEP regional aircraft, where the signal is used to assess the size of the vertical tail.

</details>

Abstract: The digitization of protection relays exposes power grids to sophisticated cyberattacks, yet existing GNN detectors omit temporal encoding, rely on static topologies, and lack relay localization. We propose PTMTD-a Pearson-driven Temporal Multimodal Topology-aware Detection Network-coupling a dilated TCN with multi-head GATv2 in a dual-head architecture for simultaneous 37-class detection and four-relay localization. PTMTD constructs its graph from measurement correlations (no impedance data) and is trained with a composite loss jointly optimizing both tasks. On the MSU-ORNL benchmark (78,377 samples, 37 classes), it achieves 99.27% accuracy, 99.25% macro F1, and 95.98% localization, outperforming GraphKAN, LSTM, GCN, and Random Forest by 5.64-11.15 percentage points (p < 0.05). Ablations confirm gains from temporal encoding (+2.38%), graph attention (+2.69%), residuals (+2.07%), and correlation-driven supervision. With 639k parameters and 2.5 ms CPU inference, PTMTD is lightweight and deployment-ready. While validated on a transmission testbed, the architecture applies to microgrids with similar relay structures. Code and models are publicly available.

</details>

Abstract: This paper addresses the data-driven synchronization and robust control of multivariable chaotic systems subject to strict actuation constraints, modeling uncertainties and bounded input disturbances. Using empirical data collected from a four-dimensional chaotic Rossler response system, Sequentially Thresholded Least Squares (STLSQ) sparse regression is employed to recover a nominal control-affine model, achieving an identification accuracy of R^2 = 1.0000 across all channels with root-mean-square error within [4.15e-6, 8.33e-4]. To establish a mathematically rigorous bridge between data-driven identification and robust synthesis, all structural discrepancies in the drift vector field and input matrix are unified into a single lumped uncertainty field delta(x,u). A computable scalar bound delta_bar is established based on library truncation error, regression residuals, input bounds, and Lipschitz constants (Lemma 1). We subsequently prove that input-output feedback linearization maps delta(x,u) into a state- and control-dependent additive disturbance d(z,nu) in Brunovsky canonical coordinates, bounded by ||d(z,nu)||_2 <= alpha_1 delta_bar + alpha_2 delta_bar ||nu||_2 with closed-form expressions for alpha_1 and alpha_2 (Theorem 1). This certified bound, together with a bounded input disturbance and a zero-order-hold margin, yields a compact disturbance set that seeds a robust tube-based model predictive controller. We prove that the resulting tightened quadratic program is recursively feasible, that the physical actuator limit (|u_i| <= 1100) remains inactive under an explicit sufficient condition, and that the closed-loop tracking error is Input-to-State Stable (ISS) with an asymptotic gain that is linear in delta_bar plus a term due to the exogenous input disturbance (Theorems 2 and 3).

</details>

Abstract: Mathematical proofs in scientific papers are often long and intricate, relying on chains of references to earlier results. This makes it difficult for readers and reviewers to verify each and every step. Interactive theorem proving offers a complement: the theorem and its proof are written as code, and every inference is checked by a small, trusted piece of software called the kernel. This paper serves as an introduction to the Lean 4 proof assistant for a system identification and control audience, assuming no prior exposure to formal methods. We first show how a Lean proof is read using two elementary examples: the first on identifiability, the second on set invariance. We then illustrate how Lean can be used to analyse results from the literature, taking as example a classic lemma on the inversion of the noise filter in prediction-error identification (Ljung, 1999, Lemma 3.1). Since in Lean every step must be justified, the formalization brings to light a subtle point: the original claim of that lemma holds almost surely, although not for every realisation, as its statement may suggest. A simple counterexample makes this concrete. Finally, the paper discusses guidelines and perspectives on the use of Lean in system identification research.

</details>

Abstract: Modern power systems rely on power converters that exhibit multifrequency periodic dynamics, such as modular multilevel converters (MMCs). Such converters are commonly analysed using harmonic state-space (HSS) and dynamic phasor (DP) representations. Both approaches rely on building state matrices based on the system topology, whose size grows with the number of harmonics retained in the model. This paper shows that a symmetrical direct-quadrature (dq) dynamic phasor formulation for time-domain simulation, named DQsym, has a smaller solver state matrix than HSS and DP, and whose size is independent of the truncation order K. This reduction results from evaluating harmonic coupling outside the state matrix, in the nonlinear signal blocks. The open-loop MMC model used in this paper is derived in three harmonic-domain (HD) formulations and validated against a three-phase time-domain reference model. The models are compared in terms of state-space size and sparsity, harmonic content, steady-state accuracy and eigenvalues. For truncation at K = 4, the DQsym solver has 45 states against 135 for HSS and DP, stays within about 4% of the reference, and, once closed with its coupling blocks, yields eigenvalues identical to those of HSS and DP.

</details>

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a reinforcement learning (RL) approach to space-filling input design for nonlinear system identification, in which an agent learns a control policy that actively drives the system to explore its state space. Classical input design, developed mainly for linear models, minimises the covariance of the estimated parameters. For nonlinear black-box models, however, the dominant error is the bias induced by the model structure over the region of interest, which motivates space-filling strategies that seek to cover the system state space. Existing space-filling approaches rely on predefined input signal classes and require solving explicit optimisation problems. Unlike these methods, the proposed approach requires no input class to be selected and no frequency band to be tuned for the system at hand, satisfies an amplitude bound by construction, and adapts to varying initial conditions through its observation-based policy without re-optimisation. The learned excitation signals exhibit physically meaningful behaviours, such as resonance excitation and amplitude modulation. The approach is evaluated against tuned swept sine and crest-factor-optimised multisine excitations, and against a state-of-the-art space-filling multisine design, on two nonlinear benchmark systems: a mass-spring-damper with nonlinear stiffness and a Van der Pol oscillator. Under the same amplitude bound, the learned policy achieves the best overall space-filling performance on both systems and distributes its samples more uniformly than the state-of-the-art design.

</details>

Abstract: Urban mobility systems are large-scale networked systems whose performance emerges from hierarchically coupled decisions of public authorities, competing mobility operators, and travelers. Existing approaches to policy design either collapse one of these levels or assume that the authority can model and differentiate through the equilibrium responses of operators and travelers. In this paper, we model policy design as a tri-level game on a multimodal network, in which a municipality sets taxes, licenses, and integration subsidies, a public transport and a taxi operator compete on prices and service supply, and heterogeneous travelers reach a stochastic user equilibrium. We prove that the traveler equilibrium is unique and continuously differentiable in the operator strategies, that the variational inequality characterizing operator equilibria admits a solution for every policy, and that the resulting tri-level game admits a Stackelberg equilibrium. Building on this structure, we design a model-free hierarchical controller in which the municipality and the operators update their decisions from zeroth-order evaluations of observed equilibrium outcomes. On the Zurich multimodal network, operator equilibria computed from different initializations differ by a median of 0.3% in the municipal objective. On a grid of 3,125 policies, only those that subsidize transfers between public transport and taxis benefit both operators, and 24 of them also lower travel costs without degrading the municipal objective. Finally, the controller matches the best policies of three black-box optimizers and terminates at the most consistent policies across runs.

</details>

Abstract: We present a control strategy for power management of a variable-speed 15MW (rated power) centrifugal compressor, and investigate how quickly it can follow a reduced electrical power reference without entering surge. We use a Greitzer-Gravdahl type model scaled to industrial size with rate-limited valve actuators as a simulation environment and propose an advanced regulatory control structure using feedback control loops with dynamically scaled feedforward mechanisms and a structure of min/max selectors. In simulated experiments, a 6MW power reduction from varying initial operating points on a 12MW iso-power line is delivered at rates of up to 10MW/s, i.e.to 90% in 0.54s, without opening the recycle valve. Depending on the initial operating point, a large power reduction can be delivered at once or with a restricted ramp rate. The stroking rate of the discharge side valve is identified as the limiting factor for how fast the power can be reduced in the studied configuration. A limited sensitivity study on the impact of rotor inertia for power shedding performance is also provided.

Abstract: Pseudo-inverse methods are widely used to solve control allocation problems for overactuated aircraft with redundant control effectors. These methods reliably satisfy the primary command tracking objective, while the remaining null-space degrees of freedom are in principle available for secondary objectives such as drag reduction or actuator wear balancing. However, exploiting this freedom in practice requires hand-tuned weighting matrices or a priori preferred deflections that are difficult to specify across a wide flight envelope with nonlinear, state-dependent dynamics. This paper presents a hybrid architecture in which a reinforcement learning agent operates exclusively within the null space of the control effectiveness matrix, learning secondary objective strategies directly from interaction with the flight dynamics. The null-space projector guarantees by construction that primary command tracking remains invariant regardless of the agent output, providing a principled separation between certified primary control and adaptive secondary optimization. The approach is demonstrated on a nonlinear six-degree-of-freedom distributed electric propulsion aircraft model with aero-propulsive effects across diverse mission scenarios, achieving measurable drag reduction compared to the baseline allocation while preserving tracking integrity and respecting actuator constraints. The proposed architecture forms a foundation for future extensions toward multi-objective, mission-conditioned policies.

</details>

Abstract: Control invariance for interconnected non-linear systems on manifolds requires conditions under which subsystem safety constraints can be combined to form an invariant set. This paper develops local product type control invariant sets on manifolds using tangent bundle splitting, adapted product coordinates, and subsystem tangency conditions satisfied simultaneously by one admissible input at each state. The resulting conditions establish control invariance of product sets without requiring independent subsystem dynamics, with separate control choices possible when inputs are independently constrained and each acts only on its subsystem. This provides a way to verify subsystem safety without discarding dynamic coupling and is illustrated by a torus example in which adapted coordinates give a curved constraint region a product representation.

</details>

Abstract: We present contraction-certified sampling-based model predictive control (CS-MPC) for tracking and safety of nonlinear robotic systems under parametric uncertainty, disturbances, and safety constraints that are revealed online. CS-MPC uses a contraction-certified tracking feedback inside every sampled rollout, which concentrates the rollouts around the reference, and an execution layer that verifies the applied input using a quantile-tightened discrete-time control barrier function condition. We derive a verification procedure for nonlinear contraction of the feedback-closed dynamics, and we prove a finite-sample stochastic stability bound and a mission-level probabilistic safety guarantee. We validate CS-MPC in simulation on a rover, a quadrotor, and an asteroid landing. When compared with standard model predictive path integral (MPPI) control, CS-MPC attains the same tracking error with 7 to 20 times fewer rollouts for the rover, and a 2 to 4 times lower tracking error for the quadrotor, with no safety violations in 200 Monte Carlo missions.

</details>

Abstract: Predicting the response of a nonlinear system across different feedback controllers requires a model that captures how the closed-loop dynamics change with the controller parameters. In a finite-dimensional Koopman model, these changes may affect both the latent dynamics and the observable coordinates used to represent them. In this paper, we propose a policy-conditioned Koopman representation framework to address this problem. This framework allows a single learned model to predict a family of closed-loop trajectories without retraining for each controller. We test the framework on four nonlinear prediction benchmarks, where the model stays below a 15% error threshold on every system, while each baseline exceeds it on at least one. As an example application, the resulting surrogate supports one-shot feedback-parameter selection: an optimization at the initial state selects a controller that then operates with fixed parameters and ongoing state feedback. On nonlinear control benchmarks including MuJoCo, the selected controllers stabilize every system with 3 to 97 times less online computation than nonlinear model predictive control. These results demonstrate how policy-dependent coordinates can support both prediction across closed-loop systems and controller selection without repeated online trajectory optimization.

</details>

The cases range from 250 to 78,484 buses, with geolocated substations, voltages up to 765 kV, and line parameters taken from conductor data. Each ships under an open license in three common power flow formats that agree at the solved operating point, and the six larger cases have a companion hourly, bus-level load series for one year. The Texas, Florida, and Michigan, Ohio, and Indiana cases are sub-networks of the Eastern case. Every case solves an alternating-current power flow from its stored operating point. With parallel circuits merged, the Eastern and Western cases are 9 and 16% denser than the real interconnections, while graph-statistics cases on the same footprints lie within 6% and meet more published realism criteria. A full single-outage contingency analysis completes on every case, and in an optimal power flow benchmark every case except the New England base case and one of its 12 operating points solves in polar form.

Abstract: Modern power systems are undergoing a huge shift in generation mix, including inverter-based resources that are fundamentally changing the stability dynamics of the grid. Thus, synchronous Generators (SG) are gradually being displaced by Grid-forming inverters (GFM) that are proposed as a solution to continue supporting grid stability. However, the effect of GFM placement in the network on frequency performance and how well aggregate-frequency assessment captures placement sensitivity observed in local frequency metrics remains unclear. This paper evaluates all SG and droop-controlled GFM assignments in the IEEE 39-bus system. A reduced-order model is used for the exhaustive search with load disturbances applied separately at every eligible load bus while considering nadir and Rate of Change of Frequency (RoCoF) derived from both device rating weighted aggregate frequency and local bus frequencies. Results reveal that at fixed GFM penetration, placement produced up to 104 mHz variation in worst-case local nadir and 0.21 Hz/s in maximum local RoCoF. Aggregate-frequency assessment reduced the apparent across placements by 89\% for nadir and 88\% for RoCoF at 50\% GFM penetration. These results indicate that aggregate-frequency assessment alone may obscure favorable or adverse placement effects on local frequency performance.

</details>

Abstract: 2D-to-3D image registration is a key component for several medical imaging purposes such as intraoperative guidance, enabling the alignment between preoperative 3D volumes and real-time 2D images. While conventional optimisation-based methods often struggle with non-convex objectives, sensitivity to initialisation, and high computational demands, deep learning (DL) has emerged as a promising alternative, offering improved alignment robustness and the potential for real-time performance. This review provides a comprehensive analysis of DL frameworks developed to address the challenges inherent in 2D-to-3D registration. We first outline how core concepts of DL are adapted to the registration problem. We then examine recent methodological advancements from multiple perspectives, including pipeline design, architectural innovations, and domain adaptation strategies. Particular attention is given to quantitative metrics, highlighting how performance is defined and measured across studies. Across the 41 included studies, we find that direct regression is the most common pipeline (23 studies, against 18 iterative ones), that hybrid CNN-attention architectures are gaining ground, and that research remains heavily concentrated on X-ray-to-CT registration. We conclude by identifying the absence of per-case uncertainty estimation and the strong neglect of temporal or sequential modelling as the two methodological gaps most consequential for clinical readiness, and discuss state-space models as a way to add temporal memory within intraoperative latency budgets. Further opportunities lie in more principled domain adaptation and in the integration of foundation models for representation learning.

</details>

Although recent deep learning approaches have demonstrated promising performance, many rely on whole-brain feature extraction and provide limited interpretability. This study proposes a tumour-aware multimodal framework for explainable, non-invasive prediction of IDH mutation status from multiparametric magnetic resonance imaging (MRI). The proposed framework integrates four MRI sequences (T1, T1CE, T2, and FLAIR), tumour subregion segmentation, quantitative radiomic descriptors, and clinical information within a unified deep learning architecture. An ablation study was conducted to evaluate the contribution of tumour-aware learning, radiomic features, and clinical variables. Model performance was assessed using multiple complementary evaluation metrics, including accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, balanced accuracy, receiver operating characteristic analysis, confusion matrix evaluation, and Grad-CAM visualisation. Experimental results demonstrated that the proposed multimodal framework consistently outperformed the baseline MRI-only model, with the Clinical Fusion model achieving the strongest overall predictive performance. The integration of tumour-aware learning, radiomic descriptors, and clinical information progressively improved classification performance, while Grad-CAM visualisations indicated that the model predominantly focused on biologically relevant tumour regions. These findings suggest that tumour-aware multimodal feature integration enables accurate, interpretable, and non-invasive molecular profiling of gliomas and provides a promising foundation for future clinical decision support in precision neuro-oncology.

</details>


### [267] [BeST: Beta splatted tomography for direct reconstruction from coherent imaging measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17982)
*Johannes Gruen,Philipp Paetzold,Andreas Schropp,Aknur Karabay,Shahin Sepanlou,Sebastian Eberle,Martin Seyrich,Felix Wittwer,Imke Greving,Silja Flenner,Ken Vidar Falch,Jan Garrevoet,Manuel Guizar-Sicairos,Christian G. Schroer,Johannes Hagemann*

Abstract: Conventional coherent X-ray tomography requires the phase of each projection to be reconstructed with high fidelity. However, individual reconstructed projections might be flawed, e.g. due to positioning errors, yielding inconsistent tomographic reconstructions. By directly coupling phase retrieval and tomography, consistency is enforced through a common object volume. But reconstructing a full volume demands substantial computational resources. To reduce this demand, we introduce Beta Splatted Tomography (BeST), which represents the common object volume using compactly supported beta primitives with closed-form analytical projections, enabling efficient reconstruction directly from coherent scattering images. This representation substantially reduces the number of optimizable parameters compared to a voxel grid. We demonstrate BeST on experimental datasets from hard X-ray near-field holography and far-field ptychography measurements. In both cases, the joint technique improves reconstruction quality compared to two-step methods.

</details>


### [268] [X-Strata: Separating Aneurysms and Overlapping Vessels into Layers from 2D X-ray Angiography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17743)
*Zhihong Wen,Congzhou Chen,Hucheng Li,Xun Han,Jianning Zhang,Yakun Chen,Hao Gao,Hao Zhao,Yudong Ma,Guanqiao Li,Yongping Liang,Shengli Guo*

Main category: eess.IV

Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: An X-ray angiogram sums every structure along a ray into one pixel. When vessels cross an aneurysm, they obscure part of it and mix their contrast into its filling curve. We present X-Strata, a network that separates a cine angiogram into three layers: the complete aneurysm sac, the vessels in front of it, and the vessels behind it. Contrast timing indicates how much each layer contributes to a pixel, and parallax between two views indicates their order, which a single view cannot determine. X-Strata first estimates the layers and their contrast curves, then uses the second view to assign vessels to the front or back layer, and lifts all layers into 3D. It learns from layer labels projected from same-session 3D rotational angiography, but needs only 2D cine runs and their acquisition geometry at inference. On 220 held-out patients from three centers (122 external), two-view X-Strata ranks first among 14 baselines, including VGGT and C$^2$RV, in recovering the sac where vessels overlap it (Dice 0.693 internal, 0.641 external) and in ordering the layers (balanced accuracy 0.703, 0.659). Its order accuracy exceeds that of all 11 end-to-end baselines by at least 4.1 points internally and 3.3 externally (Holm-adjusted $p < 0.001$) and grows with view separation. Recovered 3D sac surfaces have a median Chamfer distance of 0.34 mm on the internal cohort. X-Strata may therefore provide layered aneurysm and vessel information, including 3D sac shape, for patients who undergo only 2D angiography.

</details>


### [269] [Few-Shot Open-Set Hyperspectral Pixel-Wise Classification with Spatial Aware Label Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17974)

Abstract: Pixelwise classification of hyperspectral images (HSI) is a fundamental task in remote sensing, but its deployment in real-world applications is often limited by the scarcity of labeled data and the presence of unknown classes at inference time. In this work, we propose Few-shot Open-set hyperspectral recognition from Self-Supervised Learning training (FOSSeL), a unified framework for few-shot, open-set hyperspectral pixelwise classification that does not require supervised training. The proposed approach first leverages self-supervised learning to construct a discriminative feature space from unlabeled data. Then, given a small set of labeled seed pixels, class predictions are obtained through spatially-aware graph-based label propagation over superpixel representations, enabling the joint exploitation of spectral similarity and spatial consistency. To address the open-set scenario, we introduce simple yet effective strategies to identify pixels that do not belong to the known classes. We evaluate FOSSeL on three benchmark hyperspectral datasets under multiple few-shot settings. The results show that the proposed framework achieves competitive or superior performance compared to fully supervised methods while maintaining robustness in the presence of unknown classes. These findings highlight the potential of combining self-supervised feature learning with graph-based inference for practical HSI analysis in low-annotation and open-world scenarios.

</details>


### [270] [D2ToF: One-Step Diffusion for Depth Super-Resolution from Lightweight Single-Photon LiDAR Waveforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18243)
*Pallabjyoti Deka,Lewis Wilson,Stephen McLaughlin,Jonathan Leach,Abderrahim Halimi*

Abstract: Single-photon LiDAR (SPL) sensors offer exceptional temporal resolution and single-photon sensitivity for depth imaging. However, cost-effective SPL arrays typically suffer from extremely low spatial resolutions (e.g. $8\times 8$ pixels), limiting their real-world utility. In this paper, we propose a novel, one-step super-resolution framework, D2ToF, Distilled Diffusion for Time-of-Flight, that bridges the gap between high-fidelity generative modeling and real-time execution. Our approach uses an amortized variational distillation strategy that allows for a measurement-based data consistency term to be incorporated directly into the one-step training objective. This encourages that the synthesized high-resolution depth maps are consistent with the captured low-resolution Time-of-Flight (ToF) histograms. To overcome the severe ill-posedness of upsampling extremely degraded inputs, we introduce a multimodal guidance strategy. By leveraging a foundational monocular depth model to extract structural priors from a high-resolution reflectivity image, our network is guided by a rich triad of the ToF histogram, reflectivity map, and relative depth estimate. Experimental results outperform evaluated baselines, achieving $16\times$ spatial super-resolution (from $8\times 8 \times 100$ histograms to $128\times 128$ depth maps) at an accelerated inference speed of approximately $83$ milliseconds.

</details>


### [271] [DecompJSCC: Joint Source-Channel Coding With Decoupled SNR and Rate Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18379)
*Yunhao Liu,Jiancun Fan,Haojun Sun,Jie Luo,Qing Zhao*

Abstract: Deep joint source-channel coding (DeepJSCC) for image transmission must adapt to changes in the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) and available transmission resources. However, some existing joint-adaptive methods condition the encoder on the requested rate, while others apply static masks after encoding. The former couples latent formation to the rate request, whereas the latter cannot adapt channel selection to image content. To address these limitations, we propose DecompJSCC, which decomposes joint adaptation into SNR-conditioned latent formation and post-encoding rate control within a single model. The encoder applies SNR-conditioned block attention residuals across all stages, aggregating intra-stage and cross-stage semantic representations. At the receiver, an adapter performs SNR-conditioned affine correction. We introduce a post-encoding rate-adaptive channel semantic budgeting (RCSB) module whose shared gate selects channels of the formed latent by image content, adjusts their gains, and keeps exactly the requested count. This keeps the encoder output independent of the rate request for a fixed image and SNR. Experimental results show that DecompJSCC outperforms SwinJSCC and SA-RA-JSCC in peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) across a range of SNR and rate settings, by 0.64 and 1.00 dB over SwinJSCC and by 0.97 and 0.96 dB over SA-RA-JSCC on Kodak and CLIC2021, while using only 7.5% of SwinJSCC's Adaptive ModNet parameters.

</details>
